宿主 细胞进入受体作为呼吸道病毒感染的药理学靶点:从生物学到临床转化

Host cell entry receptors as pharmacological targets in respiratory viral infections: from biology to clinical translation
作者： Sherif A. El‐Kafrawy, Norah A. Othman, Mustafa Zeyadi, Mai M. El-Daly, Esam I. Azhar, Sherif A. El-Kafrawy
摘要中文翻译由季节性流感、呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 和潜在大流行的冠状病毒引起的呼吸道病毒感染每年导致数百万人住院,估计有数十万人死亡,2024-2025 年严重的流感季节和人畜共患威胁(如进化枝 2.3.4.4b H5N1)的持续出现加剧了这一负担。本综述批判性地研究了靶向 宿主 细胞表面受体作为 治疗 和预防呼吸道病毒感染的策略的药理学基础,评估了每个主要受体轴的当前证据,并确定了必须解决的关键翻译差距。呼吸道病毒,包括流感、RSV、SARS-CoV-2 和 MERS-CoV,每年总共导致数百万人住院,抗原漂移、人畜共患疾病的出现和 抗病毒药物 耐药性等持续挑战突出表明需要采取机制上不同的策略。针对保守受体(ACE2、DPP4、唾液酸、TMPRSS2)的 宿主 定向疗法 (HDT) 利用病毒进入所需的遗传稳定的 宿主 因子。在此回顾的受体轴中,我们评估了十多种从临床前开发到 III 期评估的候选药物,包括 TMPRSS2 抑制剂、可溶性 ACE2 诱饵、抗 CD147 和受体阻断单克隆抗体、唾液酸酶融合蛋白 DAS181 和禽类 IgY 制剂。这一证据揭示了强大的临床前活性与迄今为止有限的临床疗效之间的一致差距。我们得出的结论是,宿主 受体靶向是呼吸 抗病毒药物 景观中机械合理但尚未得到临床证实的组成部分;它的价值最有可能在特定的领域(预防、早期门诊 治疗 以及与直接作用的 抗病毒药物 组合)以及大流行防范框架内实现,前提是此处确定的安全性、交付和试验设计挑战得到解决。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述研究了 宿主 细胞表面受体的药理学靶向作为治疗和预防呼吸道病毒感染的策略,重点关注 禽流感 A 病毒(例如 H5N1、H5N6、H7N9)和其他人畜共患呼吸道病原体。核心问题涉及针对 宿主 和相关人畜共患威胁的保守受体(如 ACE2、DPP4、唾液酸和 TMPRSS2)的 宿主 定向疗法 (HDT) 的转化潜力。
证据基础与方法
该综述是一篇叙述性综合文章,批判性地评估了针对呼吸道病毒感染中 宿主 细胞受体的药理学基础,评估了临床前和临床阶段的候选药物。它确定了翻译差距,但不报告系统检索日期、资格标准或纳入研究计数。
共识与结论
该综述的结论是,宿主 受体靶向在机制上是合理的,但在呼吸道病毒感染方面尚未得到临床证实。它强调了跨受体轴(包括 TMPRSS2 抑制剂、可溶性 ACE2 诱饵、抗 CD147 抗体和唾液酸酶融合蛋白)的强大临床前活性和有限的临床疗效之间的一致差距。
争议与缺口
该审查确定了将临床前疗效转化为临床结果方面尚未解决的挑战,包括安全性问题、交付障碍和试验设计限制。它指出,虽然 HDT 以保守的 宿主 因子为目标,但其临床价值尚未得到证实,特别是在大流行防范框架中。
科研与实践启示
该审查优先考虑解决安全性、交付和试验设计挑战,以实现 宿主 受体靶向在预防、早期门诊 治疗 以及与直接作用 抗病毒药物 组合方面的潜力。它强调大流行病防范框架需要整合 HDT,这取决于解决已确定的转化差距。

Host cell entry receptors as pharmacological targets in respiratory viral infections: from biology to clinical translation

Authors: Sherif A. El‐Kafrawy, Norah A. Othman, Mustafa Zeyadi, Mai M. El-Daly, Esam I. Azhar, Sherif A. El-Kafrawy
Original abstract (English)
Respiratory viral infections caused by seasonal influenza, respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), and pandemic-potential coronaviruses are responsible for several million hospitalisations and an estimated several hundred thousand deaths each year, a burden underscored by the severe 2024–2025 influenza season and the continued emergence of zoonotic threats such as clade 2.3.4.4b H5N1. This review critically examines the pharmacological basis for targeting host cell-surface receptors as a strategy for the treatment and prophylaxis of respiratory viral infections, evaluates the current evidence for each major receptor axis, and identifies the key translational gaps that must be addressed. Respiratory viruses, including influenza, RSV, SARS-CoV-2, and MERS-CoV, collectively cause millions of hospitalisations annually, and the persistent challenges of antigenic drift, zoonotic emergence, and antiviral resistance highlight the need for mechanistically distinct strategies. Host-directed therapies (HDTs) targeting conserved receptors (ACE2, DPP4, sialic acids, TMPRSS2) exploit genetically stable host factors required for viral entry. Across the receptor axes reviewed here, we appraise more than a dozen candidate agents spanning preclinical development through Phase III evaluation, including TMPRSS2 inhibitors, soluble ACE2 decoys, anti-CD147 and receptor-blocking monoclonal antibodies, the sialidase fusion protein DAS181, and avian IgY preparations. This evidence reveals a consistent gap between robust preclinical activity and as-yet-limited clinical efficacy. We conclude that host receptor targeting is a mechanistically rational but still clinically unproven component of the respiratory antiviral landscape; its value is most plausibly realised in defined niches—prophylaxis, early outpatient treatment, and combination with direct-acting antivirals—and within pandemic preparedness frameworks, provided that the safety, delivery, and trial-design challenges identified here are resolved.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines pharmacological targeting of host cell-surface receptors as a strategy for treating and preventing respiratory viral infections, focusing on avian influenza A viruses (e.g., H5N1, H5N6, H7N9) and other zoonotic respiratory pathogens. The central question addresses the translational potential of host-directed therapies (HDTs) targeting conserved receptors such as ACE2, DPP4, sialic acids, and TMPRSS2 for avian influenza and related zoonotic threats.
Evidence base and method
The review is a narrative synthesis that critically evaluates the pharmacological basis for targeting host cell receptors in respiratory viral infections, appraising candidate agents across preclinical and clinical stages. It identifies translational gaps but does not report systematic search dates, eligibility criteria, or included-study counts.
Consensus and conclusions
The review concludes that host receptor targeting is mechanistically rational but remains clinically unproven for respiratory viral infections. It highlights a consistent gap between robust preclinical activity and limited clinical efficacy across receptor axes, including TMPRSS2 inhibitors, soluble ACE2 decoys, anti-CD147 antibodies, and sialidase fusion proteins.
Controversies and gaps
The review identifies unresolved challenges in translating preclinical efficacy to clinical outcomes, including safety concerns, delivery barriers, and trial-design limitations. It notes that while HDTs target conserved host factors, their clinical value remains unproven, particularly in pandemic preparedness frameworks.
Research and practice implications
The review prioritizes resolving safety, delivery, and trial-design challenges to realize the potential of host receptor targeting in prophylaxis, early outpatient treatment, and combination with direct-acting antivirals. It emphasizes the need for pandemic preparedness frameworks to integrate HDTs, contingent on addressing identified translational gaps.

H5N1 avian flu (bird flu) - Yarra Ranges Council
新闻简报亚拉山脉委员会于 2026 年 8 月 19 日在维多利亚州确认了 H5N1 禽流感病例。该委员会提供了有关 暴发 的信息,但没有具体说明病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数。该事件被列为已确认事件,该委员会强调需要访问维多利亚农业部以获取最新消息。没有报告有关影响或风险规模的详细信息。响应状态包括一般信息传播,但证据中没有概述测试、隔离或接触者追踪等具体行动。该委员会的通报强调了该地区存在该病毒,并鼓励居民从官方渠道寻求进一步指导。

A high priorityPublic-health news

H5N1 avian flu (bird flu) - Yarra Ranges Council

News Brief
Yarra Ranges Council confirmed cases of H5N1 avian flu (bird flu) in Victoria on 19 August 2026. The council provided information about the outbreak but did not specify the number of cases, deaths, or exposures. The event is classified as confirmed, and the council emphasized the need to visit Agriculture Victoria for the latest updates. No details about the scale of impact or risk were reported. The response status includes general information dissemination, but no specific actions such as testing, isolation, or contact tracing were outlined in the evidence. The council's communication highlights the presence of the virus in the region and encourages residents to seek further guidance from official sources.

Published: 2026-08-15  |  Publisher: NSW Department of Primary Industries
A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

H5N1 禽流感(H5 禽流感) - 新南威尔士州第一产业部

H5N1 Avian influenza (H5 bird flu) - NSW Department of Primary Industries
新闻简报新南威尔士州第一产业部证实,澳大利亚南部海岸的几种流浪迁徙海鸟体内存在 H5N1 禽流感(H5 禽流感)。这是一个重大的生物安全事件,因为 H5N1 是 禽流感 的高致病性菌株。该部门正在通过 BioResponse NSW 应用程序实时更新来积极管理局势,并鼓励公众报告任何害虫或疾病。虽然没有提供具体病例或死亡人数,但确认强调了在监测 禽流感 暴发 时保持警惕的必要性。该部门还通过专门的热线和资源解决其他生物安全问题,例如牛蜱和蜱热。本报告强调了新南威尔士州为保障动物健康和福利所做的持续努力。

新南威尔士州第一产业部正在提供有关生物安全紧急情况的信息,包括 H5N1 禽流感 暴发。他们还推出了 BioResponse NSW 应用程序进行实时更新,并鼓励报告害虫或疾病。
A high priorityPublic-health news

H5N1 Avian influenza (H5 bird flu) - NSW Department of Primary Industries

News Brief
The NSW Department of Primary Industries confirmed the presence of H5N1 avian influenza (H5 bird flu) in several vagrant migratory seabirds on Australia's southern coast. This is a significant biosecurity event, as H5N1 is a high pathogenicity strain of avian influenza. The department is actively managing the situation through real-time updates via the BioResponse NSW app and encouraging the public to report any pests or diseases. While no specific case or death counts are provided, the confirmation highlights the need for vigilance in monitoring avian influenza outbreaks. The department is also addressing other biosecurity issues, such as cattle tick and tick fever, through dedicated hotlines and resources. This report underscores the ongoing efforts to safeguard animal health and welfare in NSW.

南澳大利亚州发现 1,000 多只禽鸟死于疑似 H5N1 暴发

Over 1,000 birds found dead in suspected H5N1 outbreak in South Australia
新闻简报据 2026 年 8 月 20 日星期四报道,在南澳大利亚州,在偏远近海岛屿发生的三起疑似 H5N1 死亡率 大规模 死亡率 事件中,发现超过 1,000 只禽类死亡。州政府正在严重关注这一情况,因为这些死亡事件代表该地区禽流感的严重升级。此外,袋鼠岛上发生的一起涉及小企鹅的疑似病例进一步引起了人们的警惕。州长 Peter Malinauskas 强调需要采取保护措施,包括针对脆弱的本土鸟类种群采取 疫苗接种 措施。暴发仍在调查中,具体影响和风险级别仍有待确定。有证据表明,H5N1 病毒在该地区很活跃,尚未出现确诊病例,但对野生动物的潜在威胁是公认的。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 20 日星期四,南澳大利亚报告了疑似 H5N1 死亡率,2026 年 8 月 19 日星期三,在偏远的近海岛屿上发生了大规模 死亡率 事件。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在南澳大利亚,特别是在偏远的近海岛屿上。受影响的种群包括凤头燕鸥和小企鹅,截至 2025 年 10 月,袋鼠岛上估计有 704 只正在繁殖的小企鹅。
事件
据新华社和澳大利亚广播公司报道,在南澳大利亚偏远近海岛屿发生的三起疑似H5N1 禽流感大规模死亡率事件中,发现1000多只鸟类死亡。该事件是可疑的,但尚未得到证实。
规模、影响与风险
超过 1,000 只鸟类因疑似 H5N1 暴发 死亡,另外一起涉及小企鹅的疑似病例也令人担忧。该事件规模重大,州政府正在以最严肃的态度对待。明确指出该风险是相关的,但没有提供具体的风险级别。
应对、状态与不确定性
南澳大利亚州州长 Peter Malinauskas 表示,当局将采取一切实际和适当的行动来保护该州的野生动物,并正在考虑针对圈养或管理的脆弱本地鸟类种群进行 疫苗接种。回应正在进行中并正在调查中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Over 1,000 birds found dead in suspected H5N1 outbreak in South Australia

News Brief
In South Australia, over 1,000 birds were found dead in three suspected H5N1 avian influenza mass mortality events on remote offshore islands, reported on Thursday, 20 August 2026. The state government is treating the situation with serious concern, as the deaths represent a significant escalation of bird flu in the region. Additionally, a suspected case involving a little penguin on Kangaroo Island has raised further alarm. The Premier, Peter Malinauskas, emphasized the need for protective measures, including targeted vaccination of vulnerable native bird populations. The outbreak is still under investigation, and the exact impact and risk level remain to be determined. The evidence suggests that the H5N1 virus is active in the area, with no confirmed cases yet, but the potential threat to wildlife is acknowledged.
Original source text (English)

South Australian Premier Peter Malinauskas told the state parliament that the flocks of crested terns were found dead by surveillance teams on remote offshore islands on Wednesday. He said the deaths represented a significant escalation of bird flu in the state. "The loss of more than 1,000 birds is deeply concerning, and the state government is treating these events with the utmost seriousness,” Malinauskas said. SA is the centre of Australia's H5N1 outbreak, with 177 confirmed cases of H5 bird flu recorded as of Thursday, according to the Australian Broadcasting Corporation. In addition to the mass mortality events, Malinauskas said on Thursday that authorities were particularly concerned about a suspected case involving a little penguin found on Kangaroo Island. A survey conducted in October 2025 estimated 704 adult were breeding little penguins on the island, up from 530 in 2024. #national:禽流感防御增强:新拨款支持面临 H5N1 的家禽养殖户

#national: Bird Flu Defence Boost: New Grant Backs Poultry Farmers Facing H5N1
新闻简报New Zealand’s government has launched a new grant to bolster poultry-farm biosecurity as H5N1 bird flu becomes established in wild birds, marking a shift from prevention to long-term management. The move follows the country’s first detection of H5N1 in July 2026 in a brown skua at Petone Beach, Wellington, with no evidence of transmission to commercial poultry at that time. New regulations requiring farms with more than 100 birds to develop, register, and implement biosecurity plans—covering infection prevention, culling, disposal, and auditing—are expected to take effect by the end of 2026. The poultry sector, valued at $2.2 billion domestically and $200 million in exports, faces substantial financial risks; an earlier unrelated outbreak cost an estimated $25 million. Industry will contribute 40% of H5N1 response costs under a formal cost-sharing agreement with the government.
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 18 日发布;事件时间表涵盖 2026 年 7 月检测到 2026 年底监管实施。
地点与对象
新西兰;全国范围,重点关注全国家禽经营;惠灵顿地区(Petone Beach)进行指标检测。
事件
政府宣布一项新拨款,用于加强家禽养殖场的生物安全和 高致病性禽流感 H5N1 的长期管理;预计到 2026 年底,法规将要求为超过 100 只禽类的农场制定生物安全计划。
规模、影响与风险
H5N1 confirmed in a brown skua at Petone Beach, Wellington, in July 2026; no evidence of transmission to commercial poultry at that time; earlier unrelated outbreak in Otago cost ~$25 million; industry contributes 40% of H5N1 response costs; sector valued at ~$2.2 billion domestically and ~$200 million in exports.
应对、状态与不确定性
正在制定法规,预计将于 2026 年底实施;正式的行业与政府协议规定了应对成本分摊;重点从排除转向准备和长期管理;野生鸟类 和潜在的 跨物种溢出 向国内鸡群的未来传播仍存在不确定性。

Featured News News #national: Bird Flu Defence Boost: New Grant Backs Poultry Farmers Facing H5N1 The Government is stepping up support for New Zealand’s poultry industry as farmers prepare for the growing threat posed by H5N1 bird flu. A new grant has been launched to help poultry operators strengthen their biosecurity and prepare their farms for the long-term management of highly pathogenic avian influenza. The move comes after H5N1 was... Matthew Tukaki 0 6 Share on Facebook Share on X Share on Threads Share on Telegram The Government is stepping up support for New Zealand’s poultry industry as farmers prepare for the growing threat posed by H5N1 bird flu. A new grant has been launched to help poultry operators strengthen their biosecurity and prepare their farms for the long-term management of highly pathogenic avian influenza. The move comes after H5N1 was detected in New Zealand for the first time in July, when the globally circulating strain was confirmed in a brown skua found at Petone Beach in Wellington. There was no evidence at the time of transmission into commercial poultry. The arrival of H5N1 has changed the country’s approach from preparing for whether the virus will arrive to preparing for the possibility it becomes established among wild birds. Biosecurity Minister Andrew Hoggard has already announced new regulations requiring poultry operations with more than 100 birds to develop, register and implement farm biosecurity plans covering avian disease. Those plans will need to address how farms prevent infection and what happens if disease is detected, including arrangements for humane culling, disposal, cleaning and disinfection. Operators will also face record-keeping and auditing requirements. The Government expects the regulations to come into force by the end of 2026. The poultry sector is particularly important to New Zealand’s food supply and economy. The industry generates an estimated $2.2 billion domestically each year and around $200 million in export revenue. That makes preventing major outbreaks important not only for farmers but also for consumers, food security and trade. The Government and poultry industry already have a formal agreement setting out how they will jointly prepare for and respond to serious exotic poultry diseases. Under that arrangement, the industry contributes 45 percent of general poultry disease readiness and response costs. For an H5N1 response, the industry contribution is 40 percent, with the Government meeting the remaining share of agreed response costs. The potential financial consequences are substantial. An earlier avian influenza outbreak at a commercial egg farm in Otago cost an estimated $25 million, despite being contained to a single property. That outbreak involved a different strain from the H5N1 virus now detected in New Zealand. The new support comes as farmers face the prospect of investing more heavily in farm infrastructure and disease prevention while maintaining the supply of chicken and eggs to New Zealand households. The challenge is particularly significant for smaller poultry operations, which may have fewer resources available to make rapid biosecurity improvements. For the Government, the objective is to strengthen those defences before H5N1 becomes widespread among wild birds and creates a constant infection risk for domestic poultry. With bird flu now inside New Zealand’s borders, biosecurity is no longer simply about keeping the disease out. The focus is increasingly on ensuring farmers, food producers and communities are prepared to live with the risk while protecting flocks, livelihoods and one of the country’s most important sources of affordable protein. #BirdFlu #H5N1 #AvianInfluenza #PoultryNZ #PoultryIndustry #ChickenNZ #EggsNZ #Biosecurity #BiosecurityNZ #AndrewHoggard #MPI #PrimaryIndustries #Farmers #MaoriFarmers #MaoriAgribusiness #WhenuaMaori #FoodSecurity #KaiSecurity #AffordableKai #FoodPrices #FoodSupply #AgricultureNZ #FarmingNZ #RuralNZ #AnimalHealth #FarmBiosecurity #PoultryFarmers #NewZealandFarming #NZEconomy #Aotearoa #MaoriNews #RuralNews #NZNews #NewZealandNews #WaateaNews #RadioWaatea #WaateaDigital Author Matthew Tukaki Share with / Share on Facebook Share on X Share on Telegram Share on Threads Share on Pinterest 0 / Share on Facebook Share on X Share on Threads Share on Telegram 0% More posts. Published 18 August 2026; event timeline spans July 2026 detection to end-2026 regulatory implementation.
Location and population
New Zealand; national scope with focus on poultry operations nationwide; Wellington region (Petone Beach) for index detection.
Event
Government announced a new grant to strengthen poultry-farm biosecurity and long-term management of highly pathogenic avian influenza H5N1; regulations requiring biosecurity plans for farms with >100 birds are expected by end-2026.
Scale, impact and risk
H5N1 confirmed in a brown skua at Petone Beach, Wellington, in July 2026; no evidence of transmission to commercial poultry at that time; earlier unrelated outbreak in Otago cost ~$25 million; industry contributes 40% of H5N1 response costs; sector valued at ~$2.2 billion domestically and ~$200 million in exports.
Response, status and uncertainty

第一波禽流感是最严重的:澳大利亚可以从海外学到什么 - The Conversation

The first wave of bird flu is the worst: what Australia can learn from overseas - The Conversation
新闻简报该文章讨论了 H5N1 禽流感病毒株在澳大利亚的传播,强调该病毒于 2020 年开始在当地鸟类中传播,并于 2021 年底造成重大影响。第一波 暴发 被认为是最具破坏性的,据报道欧洲、北美和南美洲的多种鸟类出现大规模死亡。在阿根廷,一个群体中有 17,000 只象海豹死亡,一些鸟类的数量减少了全球数量的 50%。文章指出,虽然该病毒影响了许多物种,但并非所有种群或个人都受到同样的影响。在澳大利亚,当局正在采取措施为小企鹅等脆弱物种接种疫苗,并清除鸟类尸体以减缓传播。然而,文章强调,某些物种的恢复可能需要几十年的时间,并且 暴发 影响的全部范围仍在评估中。它还提到,澳大利亚独特的野生动物面临着额外的威胁,例如栖息地丧失和污染,这可能会加剧病毒的影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
第一波禽流感被描述为最严重的,该病毒于 2020 年开始在澳大利亚各地传播,并于 2021 年底造成重大影响。暴发 持续到 2022 年、2023 年和 2024 年,并在 2022 年和 2023 年记录了具体事件。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,特别关注留鸟和脆弱物种,如小企鹅、三明治燕鸥和阿黛尔企鹅等南极鸟类。文章还提到了新西兰和南美洲。
事件
文章讨论了 H5N1 禽流感病毒株在澳大利亚的传播,指出该病毒一直在留鸟中传播,并导致多个物种大规模死亡。它还强调了对欧洲、北美和南美野生动物的影响,并提供了受影响物种的具体例子。
规模、影响与风险
官方估计表明,澳大利亚约 20% 的鸟类和 10% 的哺乳动物种群面临着受到种群影响的高至极高风险。在南美洲,一些鸟类的数量减少了全球数量的 50%,而在阿根廷,单个群体中就有 17,000 只象海豹死亡。第一波浪潮被描述为毁灭性的,在繁殖年龄的成年鸟类中 死亡率 率很高,并且某些物种的种群数量大幅减少。
应对、状态与不确定性
澳大利亚当局正在采取措施,例如为圈养和严密管理的人群接种疫苗。文章还提到清除鸟类尸体以减缓病毒的传播。不过,文章指出,暴发 的全面影响仍在评估中,受影响物种的恢复可能需要数十年时间。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

The first wave of bird flu is the worst: what Australia can learn from overseas - The Conversation

News Brief
The article discusses the spread of the H5N1 strain of bird flu in Australia, highlighting that it began circulating in resident birds in 2020 and caused significant impacts by late 2021. The first wave of the outbreak is described as the most devastating, with mass deaths reported in various bird species across Europe, North America, and South America. In Argentina, 17,000 elephant seals died in a single colony, and some bird species lost up to 50% of their global population. The article notes that while the virus has affected many species, not all populations or individuals were impacted equally. In Australia, authorities are taking steps to vaccinate vulnerable species like little penguins and are removing bird carcasses to slow the spread. However, the article emphasizes that recovery for some species may take decades, and the full extent of the outbreak's impact is still being assessed. It also mentions that Australia's unique wildlife faces additional threats, such as habitat loss and pollution, which could exacerbate the effects of the virus.

Home Arts + Culture Economy Education Environment + Energy Ethics + Religion Health Politics + Society Science + Tech World Podcasts Local En Español Edition Africa Australia Brasil Canada Canada (français) Català España Europe France Global Indonesia New Zealand United Kingdom United States Donate Skip to content Edition: United States Africa Australia Brasil Canada Canada (français) Català España Europe France Global Indonesia New Zealand United Kingdom s Donate Newsletters Become an author Sign up as a reader Sign in Academic rigor, journalistic flair Arts + Culture Economy Education Environment + Energy Ethics + Religion Health Politics + Society Science + Tech World Podcasts Local jared lloyd/Getty The first wave of bird flu is the worst: what Australia can learn from overseas Published: August 20, 2026 1:09am EDT Hundreds of dead birds are now being reported as the lethal H5N1 strain of bird flu begins to spread across Australia. Government reports show the virus is circulating in resident birds. It was a long time coming. The current outbreak emerged in 2020. By late 2021, it was spreading widely, leaving a widespread trail of devastation among wildlife species across Europe and the Americas. Even as Antarctic birds and mammals began suffering over 2024 and 2025, Australia – and New Zealand – remained free of this viral strain. Until now. It’s easy to picture bird flu as an unstoppable wave set to decimate Australian birds and mammals. But that’s not set in stone. Overseas evidence shows the first wave tends to be by far the worst. And deaths from the first wave can vary greatly, with many populations and individuals surviving. That’s good news for some – but it won’t save vulnerable species who may lose a large proportion of their global population. For them, recovery is likely to take decades – if at all. The first wave is devastating In 1996, a new highly pathogenic strain of bird flu emerged in poultry in China. Periodic outbreaks forced mass poultry culls and sporadic die-offs in wild birds. In 2020, a new variant – formally known as H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b – was detected. Soon, it was sweeping through Europe and North America. It was immediately clear the virus had become more devastating. Less dangerous strains of bird flu (termed low pathogenic avian influenza viruses) tend to replicate in a bird’s gastrointestinal tract, while earlier high pathogenic strains tended to replicate in the respiratory tract. But this new strain could infect both. Not only that, it was able to infect the central nervous system, leading to neurological disease and death. The strain has evolved many new variants since 2021. These can now infect over 500 wild bird species, of which over 50% had not had infections previously recorded. Then came reports of fatal infections in mammals, from scavengers such as foxes to herbivores such as deer and marine mammals such as sea lions. Many had never had bird flu before. The virus was even able to infect livestock such as cattle. Human infections are thankfully rare, with no reports of the virus spreading between people so far. Across western Europe and North America, deaths of breeding-age adult birds were as high as 25–76% in the worst-hit species. Many populations had near-complete breeding failures. In 2023, it hit South America. Some bird species lost up to 50% of their global population, while marine mammals suffered their first mass deaths. In Argentina, 17,000 elephant seals died in a single colony. The first wave of bird flu is often devastating. Pictured: dead cranes being removed in Germany in 2025. Christophe Gateau/picture alliance via Getty What happened next? While mass deaths of wildlife made global headlines, little media coverage has focused on what happened next. Mass deaths don’t seem to strike the same populations year after year, even though the virus is still circulating. Adult sandwich terns in western Europe died in droves in 2022. But in 2023, mortality rates in adults fell by 96%. That year’s chicks were hit hard, but those hatching in 2024 did far better. Why? Older, more vulnerable birds all died off in the first wave, and surviving terns gained some immune resistance. Younger birds previously shut out from breeding took advantage of the situation, while chicks benefited from reduced competition for food. Overall, sandwich tern numbers only fell modestly. Even so, the species is projected to take almost 20 years to recover to its pre-outbreak level. Not all populations or individuals were affected equally – even in the first wave. Atlantic seabirds breeding in large, dense colonies were hit hard. Gannets, terns and skuas all saw mass deaths. But neighbouring petrels, puffins and shearwaters nesting in burrows had far fewer deaths. This may give some protection to Australia’s burrowing little penguins. Authorities are also moving to vaccinate thousands of these penguins. For many species, bird flu is made worse by other threats. In 2024, Adéle penguins in Antarctica had to battle both bird flu and deadly avian cholera, leading to many losses. Similarly, bird flu hit African penguins already in trouble from the collapse of sardine fisheries. We can’t directly extrapolate from overseas experience. Many Australian birds and mammals are unique. Some, such as black swans, lack crucial immune defences against influenza. Removal of bird carcasses is one way to slow the spread of the virus. Pictured: workers removing dead birds from a Humboldt penguin reserve in Chile in 2023. Martin Bernetti/Getty The death toll is not set Official estimates suggest about 20% of bird and 10% of mammal populations in Australia are at high to extreme risk of population impacts from this strain. But catastrophic destruction is not guaranteed. Whether a species is hit hard or barely touched will depend on three things: level of exposure to the virus, how well it can resist infection and how well it can cope with being infected. Before this strain arrived, experts evaluated the risk for all Australian bird and mammal species, developed action plans for important sites and expanded surveillance and diagnostic systems to monitor outbreaks and viral evolution. These measures are globally recognised as the most important tools. Australia is now following New Zealand in vaccinating captive and closely-managed populations. Worldwide, no species has suffered 100% losses. That means some individuals likely survived the infection. Generally, animals can cope better with infection if they’re well fed, in a good habitat and aren’t exposed to pollution or stress. The biggest lever we can pull is tackling these other pressures, from habitat loss and plastic pollution to invasive species. We have a chance to try relieving these pressures on wildlife to give them more of a fighting chance – but the window is shrinking. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

柬埔寨确认人类 H5N1 禽流感病例,因为 H5N1 袭击了更多犹他州鸡蛋农场 - CIDRAP

Cambodia confirms human H5N1 avian flu case as H5N1 hits more Utah egg farms - CIDRAP
新闻简报Cambodia confirmed its fifth human H5N1 avian influenza case of 2026 on July 16, involving a 9-month-old girl hospitalized in intensive care. Health authorities have not yet identified the exposure source, and close contacts are receiving oseltamivir prophylaxis as a precaution. In the United States, the USDA reported a fresh H5N1 outbreak at a Utah egg farm affecting 275,100 birds, part of a broader wave that has struck 1.49 million poultry in the past month. Concurrently, H5N1 detections in cattle expanded to Utah and Idaho, with 26 new cases in the last 30 days bringing the national total to 81 for 2026. Genetic sequencing in Cambodia suggests the virus is a reassortant clade (2.3.2.1e) derived from older Cambodian and globally circulating H5N1 lineages. No human cases have been linked to the U.S. animal detections.
查看新闻五要素
时间
活动日期:2026年7月16日;发布日期:2026 年 8 月 16 日。
地点与对象
柬埔寨(国民);美国犹他州(卡什县);美国爱达荷州。
事件
柬埔寨卫生部于 2026 年确认了第五例人类 H5N1 禽流感 病例,涉及一名 9 个月大的女孩,该女孩正在住院接受重症监护。在美国,美国农业部 APHIS 报告了犹他州家禽(275,100 只家禽)中检测到 H5N1 暴发,并在犹他州和爱达荷州的牛中检测到 H5N1。
规模、影响与风险
Cambodia: 19 human H5N1 cases and 8 deaths reported in 2025; 2026 case is under investigation with no identified exposure source; contacts receiving oseltamivir prophylaxis. United States: 1.49 million birds affected by H5N1 in the past 30 days; 26 cattle confirmed H5N1 cases in the past 30 days (year total: 81).
应对、状态与不确定性
柬埔寨:调查正在进行中;感染源尚未确定;抗病毒药物 预防下的接触。美国:APHIS 报告 暴发;证据中没有报告与动物检测有关的人类病例。遗传分析表明,柬埔寨存在一个重配 H5N1 进化枝 (2.3.2.1e)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Cambodia confirms human H5N1 avian flu case as H5N1 hits more Utah egg farms - CIDRAP

News Brief
Cambodia confirmed its fifth human H5N1 avian influenza case of 2026 on July 16, involving a 9-month-old girl hospitalized in intensive care. Health authorities have not yet identified the exposure source, and close contacts are receiving oseltamivir prophylaxis as a precaution. In the United States, the USDA reported a fresh H5N1 outbreak at a Utah egg farm affecting 275,100 birds, part of a broader wave that has struck 1.49 million poultry in the past month. Concurrently, H5N1 detections in cattle expanded to Utah and Idaho, with 26 new cases in the last 30 days bringing the national total to 81 for 2026. Genetic sequencing in Cambodia suggests the virus is a reassortant clade (2.3.2.1e) derived from older Cambodian and globally circulating H5N1 lineages. No human cases have been linked to the U.S. animal detections.

Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search Support Menu Cambodia confirms human H5N1 avian flu case as H5N1 hits more Utah egg farms News brief July 16, 2026 Stephanie Soucheray, MA Topics Avian Influenza (Bird Flu) Share mswine / Flickr cc Earlier this week Cambodian officials announced the country’s fifth human H5N1 avian flu case this year, this one involving a 9-month-old girl. The girl has been hospitalized and is receiving intensive care. So far no source of infection or exposure has been found, but the child’s close contacts are being given oseltamivir (Tamiflu) as a precaution. Cambodia reported 19 human cases of H5N1 in 2025, eight of which were fatal. Most recent cases in Cambodia have involved a reassortant (2.3.2.1e) between an older H5N1 clade that has circulated in Cambodia since 2014 and the newer clade 2.3.4.4b virus that is circulating globally. Utah reports H5N1 in poultry, cattle In US news, the US Department of Agriculture's Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service (APHIS) reported for the second week in a row an egg farm H5N1 outbreak in Cache County, Utah, affecting 275,100 birds. In the past 30 days, 1.49 million birds have been hit with H5N1 in the United States. APHIS also noted several new detections of avian flu in cattle in Utah and Idaho, with each state reporting four confirmed cases this week. Twenty-six cattle in the past 30 days have been confirmed to be infected with H5N1, raising the year’s total to 81 cases. Moms who receive COVID, flu vaccines during pregnancy more likely to vaccinate their babies, study says News brief July 16, 2026 Meghan Holohan Topics Childhood Vaccines COVID-19 Influenza Vaccines Adult (non-flu) Vaccines Pressmaster / iStock Infants born to mothers vaccinated before, during, or soon after pregnancy for COVID-19 or flu were more likely to receive vaccines than those born to unvaccinated mothers, suggests a study published in Pediatrics. “The results show that maternal vaccination is strongly associated with infant vaccination in the first year of life, especially when vaccination occurred during pregnancy or shortly after delivery,” wrote the study’s authors, from Kaiser Permanente Northern California. “Improving maternal vaccine coverage during those times may improve child vaccine coverage, ensuring continuous protection in early life.” Implications for policymaking, intervention development The researchers looked at 82,533 mother-infant pairs in a retrospective cohort study of babies born from August 18, 2021, to March 31, 2024, to see how mothers’ vaccination status affected their babies’ likelihood of receiving flu or COVID-19 vaccines. The mothers of 32,370 babies (39% of the total) received a COVID-19 vaccine during pregnancy, and 43,806 babies (53%) were born to mothers who received a flu shot while pregnant. Improving maternal vaccine coverage during those times may improve child vaccine coverage, ensuring continuous protection in early life. If moms received a COVID-19 vaccine during pregnancy, their babies were 9.65 times more likely to also receive the COVID-19 vaccine. Moms who received a flu shot had babies who were 3.67 times more likely to also receive one. Babies had a 66.2% probability of getting a flu shot and a 23.4% chance of getting a COVID vaccine before they turned 1 year. “Our findings also suggest that some women may not be comfortable being vaccinated during pregnancy but are willing to vaccinate their infants; differences in attitudes toward vaccinating themselves vs their children have been observed in qualitative studies of parents, although not specifically around pregnancy,” the authors wrote. “Future studies examining the interplay of these factors will be an important part of making policy and developing interventions.” Most measles patients who sought care at Prisma Health in South Carolina were kids News brief July 16, 2026 Sarah Boden Topics Measles Most of the measles patients Prisma Health Greenville Memorial Hospital cared for during South Carolina's recent outbreak were kids aged 10 years or younger. NIH According to a letter from the health system's physicians, published yesterday in the New England Journal of Medicine, Prisma treated 81 people for measles from October 2025 to April 2026. A total of 997 cases were reported to the South Carolina Department of Public Health during the outbreak. The cases were concentrated in the Upstate region, particularly Spartanburg County, where measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccination coverage was 88.9%. The letter notes that at least 73% of those who sought care at Prisma were unvaccinated. Statewide, 93% of cases were among unvaccinated South Carolinians. The most common symptoms observed by Prisma clinicians were fever, cough or other respiratory symptoms, and the characteristic rash of flat red spots. Koplik spots, which are tiny, bluish-white clustered lesions on the inside of the mouth, were reported in a quarter of patients. Severe outcomes, lasting complications None of the 81 patients seen at Prisma Health died. Ten children and three adults were hospitalized, primarily for dehydration, respiratory problems, or pneumonia. Most hospitalized patients were sent home within 72 hours, though the report notes that two had prolonged hospitalizations due to measles encephalitis, a life-threatening inflammation of the brain. One of the two was discharged to an inpatient rehabilitation facility and "as of the time of this report is receiving ongoing outpatient therapy." The other patient recovered but continues to have headaches and a vocal tic. Within a month after being diagnosed with measles, 24 of the patients had concurrent or subsequent infections. The most common infections were pneumonia, a type of ear infection called otitis media, and sinusitis, an inflammation of the sinus tissue. Time
Event date: July 16, 2026; Publication date: August 16, 2026.
Location and population
Cambodia (national); Utah, USA (Cache County); Idaho, USA.
Event
Cambodia's Ministry of Health confirmed a fifth human H5N1 avian influenza case in 2026 involving a 9-month-old girl, who was hospitalized and receiving intensive care. In the United States, the USDA APHIS reported H5N1 outbreaks in poultry (275,100 birds) in Utah and additional H5N1 detections in cattle in Utah and Idaho.
Scale, impact and risk
Cambodia: 19 human H5N1 cases and 8 deaths reported in 2025; 2026 case is under investigation with no identified exposure source; contacts receiving oseltamivir prophylaxis. United States: 1.49 million birds affected by H5N1 in the past 30 days; 26 cattle confirmed H5N1 cases in the past 30 days (year total: 81).
Response, status and uncertainty
Cambodia: Investigation ongoing; source of infection not identified; contacts under antiviral prophylaxis. United States: APHIS reporting outbreaks; no human cases linked to animal detections reported in the evidence. Genetic analysis indicates a reassortant H5N1 clade (2.3.2.1e) circulating in Cambodia.

H5N5 禽流感 检测和暴露课程 - 传染病特别版

H5N5 Avian Influenza Testing and Exposure Lessons - Infectious Disease Special Edition
新闻简报《传染病特别版》于 2026 年 8 月 19 日发布的报告强调了美国高危成年人中存在两群侵袭性 b 型流感嗜血杆菌 (Hib) 感染。这强调了 疫苗 可预防疾病的风险已变得罕见。该报告还参考了首例H5N5 禽流感人类病例的经验教训,强调了检测和暴露监测的重要性。然而,它没有提供具体的病例或死亡人数。分析表明,该报告的重点是这些发现对公共卫生和临床实践的影响,而没有详细说明调查的现状或任何具体的预防措施。内容基于完整的原始报告正文,不包含任何联合摘要限制。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告发布于2026年8月19日,活动日期为2026年8月20日。
地点与对象
报告提到美国,重点关注属于 Hib 集群的高危成年人。
事件
在高危成年人中发现了两群侵袭性 b 型流感嗜血杆菌 (Hib),这突显了 疫苗 可预防疾病的风险,这种疾病在美国已变得罕见。这是《传染病》特刊报道的。
规模、影响与风险
该报告在高危成年人中确定了两群 Hib 感染,强调了这种 疫苗 可预防疾病的风险。没有提供具体病例或死亡人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
该报告讨论了Hib集群的识别以及从首例H5N5 禽流感人类病例中吸取的教训,但没有具体说明调查的现状或任何预防措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

H5N5 Avian Influenza Testing and Exposure Lessons - Infectious Disease Special Edition

News Brief
The report published on August 19, 2026, by the Infectious Disease Special Edition highlights two clusters of invasive Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib) infections among at-risk adults in the United States. This underscores the risk of a vaccine-preventable disease that has become rare. The report also references lessons from the first human case of H5N5 avian influenza, emphasizing the importance of testing and exposure monitoring. However, it does not provide specific case or death counts. The analysis suggests that the report is focused on the implications of these findings for public health and clinical practice, without detailing the current status of the investigation or any specific prevention measures. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

三起大规模 死亡率 事件中发现 1,000 只禽类死亡,疑似禽流感 - ABC 新闻与头条 – 澳大利亚广播公司

Bird flu suspected as 1,000 birds found dead in three mass mortality events - ABC News & Headlines – Australian Broadcasting Corporation
新闻简报野生动物倡导者警告说,澳大利亚各地发生的三起大规模 死亡率 事件,涉及约 1,000 只鸟类死亡,疑似由 禽流感 引起,并将这些事件描述为禽流感活动升级的不祥征兆。美国广播公司 (ABC) 于 2026 年 8 月 20 日发布的报告引用了倡导者的观点,但没有提供实验室确认或官方病例定义。目前还没有与这些事件相关的人类感染报告。当局尚未公布所提供证据中的检测结果、控制措施或公共卫生指南,导致疑似 暴发 的状况悬而未决。该报告将这些事件描述为初步事件,并强调需要进一步调查以确认原因并评估更广泛的生态风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 20 日发布;事件报告为发生在发布前一周(未明确日期)。
地点与对象
澳大利亚;多个地区的野生和家养鸟类种群;没有人间病例报告。
事件
媒体报道援引野生动物保护人士的话说,死亡率 发生的多起大规模 死亡率 事件疑似由 禽流感(禽流感)引起。该报告是初步的,尚未通过实验室测试所提供的证据予以证实。
规模、影响与风险
据报道,三起大规模 死亡率 事件中约有 1,000 只鸟类死亡;倡导者将风险描述为 禽流感 活动恶化和潜在更广泛生态影响的迹象。
应对、状态与不确定性
正在调查中;证据中没有详细说明官方检测结果、控制措施或公共卫生应对措施。消息来源指出了倡导者的警告,而不是确认了 暴发 的状态。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu suspected as 1,000 birds found dead in three mass mortality events - ABC News & Headlines – Australian Broadcasting Corporation

News Brief
Wildlife advocates have warned that three mass mortality events involving around 1,000 dead birds across Australia are suspected to be caused by avian influenza, describing the incidents as an ominous sign of escalating bird flu activity. The ABC report, published on 20 August 2026, cites advocates but does not provide laboratory confirmation or official case definitions. No human infections have been reported in connection with the events. Authorities have not released testing results, control measures, or public health guidance in the evidence provided, leaving the status of the suspected outbreaks unresolved. The report frames the events as preliminary and emphasizes the need for further investigation to confirm the cause and assess broader ecological risks.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

禽流感摧毁了世界各地的野生动物。哪些澳大利亚鸟类面临的风险最大? - 卫报

Bird flu has devastated wildlife around the world. What Australian birds are most at risk? - The Guardian
新闻简报自 2021 年以来在全球传播的致命 H5 禽流感 毒株已抵达澳大利亚,引发本土海鸟大规模死亡,并为标志性物种敲响了警钟。当局报告首次确认菲利普岛一只小企鹅因 H5 死亡,南澳大利亚和维多利亚州出现大规模燕鸥死亡事件。政府风险评估将银鸥标记为高风险,将已被列为易危物种的澳大利亚仙燕鸥标记为极高风险,并警告可能会出现严重的种群崩溃。专家指出,海鸥的高流动性以及白腹海雕的食腐和食鱼习性是 传播 的关键途径。作为回应,联邦政府和维多利亚州政府针对圈养和 野生鸟类 启动了 疫苗接种 计划,同时承认首次遇到 H5 对本地物种的长期影响存在不确定性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 18 日发布;事件期间涵盖 2021 年至今,具体检测时间为 2026 年 7 月。
地点与对象
澳大利亚;对银鸥(海鸥)、澳大利亚鹈鹕、白腹海雕、大凤头燕鸥、澳大利亚仙燕鸥和小企鹅的具体物种关注;维多利亚时代的菲利普岛和南澳大利亚。
事件
一名媒体解说员总结了自 2021 年以来高致病性 H5 禽流感 在澳大利亚野生动物中的传播情况,在多种本地鸟类中均确认检测到,菲利普岛首次确认一只小企鹅死亡。
规模、影响与风险
菲利普岛首次确认小企鹅H5死亡;南澳大利亚和维多利亚州大凤头燕鸥大量死亡;政府风险评估将银鸥评为高风险,将澳大利亚仙燕鸥评为极高风险;专家警告说,可能会出现严重的种群数量下降和繁殖影响;海鸥表示可能会让公众看到这种影响。
应对、状态与不确定性
联邦政府宣布了针对圈养本土鸟类的 疫苗接种 计划;在发现燕鸥后,维多利亚州政府开始为野生小企鹅接种疫苗;首次经历 H5 对本地物种的影响仍存在不确定性;专家强调,海鸥的高流动性和海雕的进食行为是 传播 的风险。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu has devastated wildlife around the world. What Australian birds are most at risk? - The Guardian

News Brief
A deadly H5 avian influenza strain circulating globally since 2021 has reached Australia, triggering mass mortalities among native seabirds and raising alarm for iconic species. Authorities report the first confirmed H5 death in a little penguin on Phillip Island and large-scale tern die-offs in South Australia and Victoria. Government risk assessments label silver gulls as high risk and Australian fairy terns—already listed as vulnerable—as very high risk, warning of possible severe population crashes. Experts cite the high mobility of seagulls and the scavenging and fish-eating habits of white-bellied sea eagles as key transmission pathways. In response, federal and Victorian governments have initiated vaccination programs for captive and wild birds, while acknowledging uncertainty about long-term impacts on native species encountering H5 for the first time.
Original source text (English)

Waterbirds are highly susceptible to bird flu because they gather in large numbers for breeding and feeding. Photograph: David Dare Parker Explainer Bird flu has devastated wildlife around the world. What Australian birds are most at risk? It’s uncertain how native populations might be affected by the deadly H5 strain but seagulls, pelicans and black swans are among those species that have experts worried Follow our Australia news live blog for latest updates Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast The deadly H5 bird flu that has devastated wildlife populations globally since 2021 is spreading across Australia. The federal government announced the first vaccination plans for captive native bird species last week and the Victorian government has moved to vaccinate wild little penguins after the first detections in terns on Phillip Island. On Saturday, the state government announced the death of a little penguin on the island, the first confirmed case of H5 bird flu in the species. But for those who don’t live in communities in southern Australia on the frontline of the highly pathogenic virus, the possibility that they will at some point see the severe consequences of H5 bird flu for native birds might not have registered. First Australian death of little penguin with H5 bird flu confirmed on Phillip Island Read more There is a level of uncertainty about how native species will be affected as they are exposed to the deadly strain for the first time. Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email Here are the birds experts are most concerned about – including some of Australia’s most common and loved species. Silver gull (seagull) The silver gull – or seagull – is in the “high risk” category of a government assessment of the potential effects of H5 bird flu on every Australian bird and mammal species. High risk means, where infections occur, the species faces potentially severe drops in population sizes or viability. When the first cases in seagulls were detected in South Australia in late July, it was viewed by experts as a new front for the spread of the virus. This is because gulls are highly mobile and found in high numbers all over the country, including wetlands, inland and cities. They gather in flocks and are scavengers – two things known to increase transmission. View image in fullscreen A silver gull in Melbourne. The species is in the ‘high risk’ category of a government assessment of the potential effects of H5 bird flu. Photograph: Christopher Hopkins/AAP They are also a bird that many Australians interact with regularly. “I really am concerned about them being one species where we will see large or mass mortality because they are scavengers and they’re also very close to our big cities,” says Prof Richard Kingsford, director of the Centre for Ecosystem Science at the University of NSW. “I think seagulls will be the species that will most likely make the impact of bird flu hit home for the public.” White-bellied sea eagle In the United States, hundreds of bald eagles have died from H5 bird flu, with severe effects on breeding populations in some areas. In Australia, the white-bellied sea eagle fills a “similar niche”, says Chris Purnell, BirdLife Australia’s wetland bird and migratory shorebird program manager. View image in fullscreen The Australian white-bellied sea eagle in flight with fish in talons. Photograph: Ken Griffiths/Alamy Like the bald eagle, white-bellied sea eagles are quite common. Their range extends from southern Australia up to south-east Asia. In some parts of the country, such as Victoria and South Australia, local populations of white-bellied sea eagles are already in decline. Purnell says the white-bellied sea eagle’s feeding behaviours – including eating potentially infected birds – could expose it to the virus. “It mostly takes fish but will also regularly take waterfowl, which has been a major factor in bald eagle deaths overseas,” Purnell says. Australian fairy tern The first signs that H5 bird flu was spreading among local birds came when dead or unwell greater crested terns – a common Australian seabird – began appearing on beaches in South Australia. The first mass mortalities from bird flu in Australia have been among greater crested terns in SA and Victoria. Tern species globally have suffered large losses from the virus. BirdLife Australia holds particular concern for another tern species – the Australian fairy tern – which is listed as vulnerable under national nature laws because it has already experienced declines. View image in fullscreen BirdLife Australia holds particular concern for the fairy tern, which is listed as vulnerable under nature laws. Photograph: Andrew P Walmsley/Alamy One of Australia’s smallest seabirds, the fairy tern can share habitat with greater crested terns and even have neighbouring colonies. But there are fewer fairy terns and a higher proportion of the population congregates across fewer sites for breeding. This means the risk H5 poses to fairy terns is greater, with the possibility not only for deaths of birds but longer-term breeding impacts. In the government risk assessment, Australian fairy terns were rated “very high”, signalling potential for very severe population reductions. Purnell points to Victoria, where “our whole population of fairy terns descended on one breeding site last year”. “You’ve got a whole regional population which could accumulate in one site,” Purnell says. “If a gull flew in, dropped the virus in there and then flew off, then that could potentially impact the whole breeding population within that site.” Bird flu has been evolving and migrating for 30 years. It is a far more deadly virus that has now arrived in Australia Read more Australian pelican Kingsford says the first sign H5 bird flu has affected Australia’s waterbirds could come through the country’s beloved pelicans. Waterbirds are highly susceptible to bird flu because they gather in large numbers for breeding and feeding. They also move vast distances between inland and coastal Australia. Flocks of sometimes tens of thousands of pelicans will breed in rookeries in inland Australia when conditions are right. View image in fullscreen ‘If it gets into these big breeding rookeries of pelicans, it will be devastating,’ Prof Richard Kingsford says. Photograph: David Dare Parker “If it gets into these big breeding rookeries of pelicans, it will be devastating,” Kingsford says. There’s also potential for pelicans to be affected in a wide range of places from the coast to the bush. “Everyone loves pelicans, so this will be really upsetting for the general public.” Black swan Australia’s black swans are considered highly susceptible to H5 bird flu because they lack some of the immune genes that have helped other waterbirds combat the virus. They can breed in isolated pairs or in rookeries. Like pelicans, they can gather in large flocks and travel vast distances, increasing the possibilities for spread of the virus. Genomic research published in 2023 found black swans lacked the necessary components to detect H5N1. They also had a heightened inflammatory response when cells were infected. View image in fullscreen Australia’s black swans are considered highly susceptible to H5 bird flu because they lack some of the immune genes that have helped other waterbirds combat the virus. Photograph: Christopher Hopkins/AAP This means if they become infected, they could suffer widespread deaths, says the University of Queensland’s Prof Kirsty Short, who led the research. “You can think of black swans as a sentinel species. If there’s H5N1 in the area, black swans are likely to succumb to the infection,” she says. “They’re such an iconic species that it’s going to be noticed.” Brolga The brolga is one of Australia’s two native crane species and has deep cultural significance for Indigenous communities. A gregarious bird, known for its courtship dance, the brolga is widespread across parts of northern and eastern Australia. But in Victoria it has been reduced to an isolated population of about 600 to 700 birds. View image in fullscreen BirdLife’s Chris Purnell says if one or two brolgas were to become infected in winter, when they flock together, ‘it could spread like wildfire’. Photograph: Neil Maclachlan/Alamy Internationally, thousands of birds in the crane family have died from H5 bird flu. During winter, brolgas can flock together in groups of hundreds of birds. Purnell says if one or two birds were to become infected during that time, “it could spread like wildfire”. “They’re a highly visible bird and known in rural communities. If they’re lost from the landscape, it will be felt within these communities.” Explore more on these topics Bird flu Birds Wildlife Animals explainers Share Reuse this content Most viewed Fat people cannot win, regardless of what we do. View five news elements
Time
Published 18 August 2026; event period covers 2021–present with specific detections in July 2026.
Location and population
Australia; species-specific concern for silver gulls (seagulls), Australian pelicans, white-bellied sea eagles, greater crested terns, Australian fairy terns, and little penguins; Victorian Phillip Island and South Australia.
Event
A media explainer summarizing the spread of highly pathogenic H5 avian influenza in Australian wildlife since 2021, with confirmed detections in multiple native bird species and the first confirmed death of a little penguin on Phillip Island.
Scale, impact and risk
First confirmed H5 death in little penguins on Phillip Island; mass mortalities among greater crested terns in South Australia and Victoria; government risk assessments rate silver gulls as high risk, Australian fairy terns as very high risk; experts warn of potential severe population declines and breeding impacts; seagulls flagged as likely to make the impact visible to the public.
Response, status and uncertainty
Federal government announced vaccination plans for captive native birds; Victorian government moved to vaccinate wild little penguins after tern detections; uncertainty remains about effects on native species experiencing H5 for the first time; experts highlight high mobility of gulls and feeding behaviors of sea eagles as transmission risks.

疫苗 推出前小企鹅因禽流感死亡

Little penguin’s bird flu death ahead of vaccine roll-out - The Nightly
新闻简报这篇文章报道了澳大利亚禽流感疫情显着升级,在三起不同的大规模死亡事件中发现了 1000 多只禽类死亡。这起被称为“小企鹅禽流感死亡”的事件凸显了 禽流感 的传播及其对公共健康的潜在影响。文章指出,在 疫苗 推出之前正在对情况进行监测,这表明公共卫生准备工作正在进行中。然而,它没有提供有关调查、测试或采取的遏制措施的具体细节。重点是病毒在鸟类中的传播及其对人类健康的影响,没有提到人类感染的确诊病例。该文章旨在警告与 禽流感 相关的风险,以及面对潜在的人畜共患 传播 时需要保持警惕。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该事件的报道日期为 2026 年 8 月 20 日,文章发表于 2026 年 8 月 18 日。禽流感在多个州蔓延,在报道发布前几天发生了大规模死亡事件。
地点与对象
该事件发生在澳大利亚,特别是跨多个州。受影响的种群包括鸟类,在三起不同的大规模死亡事件中发现了 1000 多只鸟类死亡。
事件
据报道,禽流感疫情显着升级,各州有数千只禽类死亡。该文章在 疫苗 推出之前提到了“小企鹅因禽流感死亡”,表明该病毒在野生动物中存在及其对公共健康的潜在影响。
规模、影响与风险
在三起不同的大规模死亡事件中,发现超过 1000 只鸟类死亡,这表明该病毒已严重传播。该文章强调了 禽流感 的风险,特别是在 疫苗 推出和潜在人类感染的背景下。
应对、状态与不确定性
本文讨论了禽流感的传播和即将推出的 疫苗,建议持续监测和公共卫生准备。然而,它没有提供有关调查、测试或采取的遏制措施的具体细节。

Little penguin’s bird flu death ahead of vaccine roll-out - The Nightly

News Brief
The article reports on a significant escalation in the bird flu epidemic in Australia, with over 1000 birds found dead in three separate mass death events. This incident, referred to as a 'little penguin’s bird flu death,' highlights the spread of avian influenza and its potential impact on public health. The article notes that the situation is being monitored ahead of the vaccine roll-out, indicating ongoing public health preparedness. However, it does not provide specific details on the investigation, testing, or containment measures taken. The focus is on the spread of the virus among birds and the implications for human health, with no confirmed cases of human infection mentioned. The article serves as a warning about the risks associated with avian influenza and the need for vigilance in the face of potential zoonotic transmission.

Expert Commentary: What do we know about H5 bird flu? - CSIRO

News Brief
This news report from CSIRO, published on August 18, 2026, provides expert commentary on H5 bird flu. Scientists from the Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) explain their testing findings about the virus, highlighting its presence in Australia and the importance of understanding its characteristics for disease preparedness. The report does not include specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, focusing instead on the implications of the virus for public health and scientific research. The commentary is based on the ACDP's ongoing work and does not mention any current containment or response measures. The report serves as an informative overview of the virus and its relevance to Australia's scientific and health sectors.

Expert Commentary: What do we know about H5 bird flu? Following the arrival of H5 bird flu in Australia, scientists from CSIRO's Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) explain what their testing has told us about the virus. The decades‐old ‘AI alignment problem’ has finally become a reality. Solving it won’t be easy Alignment cannot depend on one AI becoming perfectly trustworthy. It is used in offices, public buildings, homes and coffee shops - often called 'WiFi Hotspots'. The invention came out of our pioneering work in radioastronomy. Where did wifi come from? Aerogard Aerogard has been keeping flying pests at bay since the 1960s, helping prevent diseases that infect animals and humans. In 1938, Doug Waterhouse was working on a repellent for sheep blowfly. With the arrival of World War II, he turned to protecting allied troops from mosquitoes. Itching to learn more World's first Hendra vaccine In 1994, a deadly new virus threatened to stop Australia's premier horse race – the Melbourne Cup. Over 16 years, 18 outbreaks of Hendra virus were recorded. In May 2011, we announced the development of a prototype vaccine for horses. By March 2013, our scientists confirmed that horses were immune to Hendra virus six months post vaccination. This was one of our longest and most successful research initiatives. 禽流感会传染人类吗?我们怎么知道? - 对话

Is bird flu infecting humans? How would we know? - The Conversation
新闻简报文章讨论了澳大利亚禽流感(H5N1)的现状,该病毒在鸟类中传播,包括候鸟和本地 野生鸟类。截至 2026 年 8 月 16 日,澳大利亚尚未报告当前流行毒株 2.3.4.4b 的人类病例,但一名从海外旅行归来的儿童出现了相关 H5N1 毒株 (2.3.2.1a) 的病例。澳大利亚疾病控制中心评估对人类的风险为低,强调虽然人类可以通过与受感染的鸟类密切接触而感染禽流感,但该病毒不会在人与人之间传播。公共卫生当局正在积极监测潜在的人类感染,并在医院和全科诊所建立了 监测 系统。他们还建议可能接触过鸟类的个人报告生病或死亡的鸟类,并采取预防措施,例如洗手和使用适当的个人防护装备。文章强调了针对禽流感进行具体检测的重要性,以及野生动物护理人员和家禽工人等高危人群保持警惕的必要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布时间:2026 年 8 月 16 日下午 4:05(美国东部时间);2026 年 6 月,澳大利亚报告了首例 H5N1 流感病例。
地点与对象
澳大利亚;野生动物护理人员、兽医人员以及管理病鸟或死鸟的人员面临的风险最高。
事件
禽流感(H5N1)正在澳大利亚鸟类中传播,目前尚未报告该病毒株(2.3.4.4b)的确诊人类病例,但一名从海外旅行归来的儿童感染了相关的 H5N1 病毒株(2.3.2.1a)。
规模、影响与风险
数以百万计的鸟类因 H5N1 病毒死亡;澳大利亚没有 2.3.4.4b 人类病例;澳大利亚疾病控制中心评估对人类的风险为低。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生当局正在监测潜在的人类感染,并且 监测 系统已经到位,可以检测人类的禽流感。测试需要特定的怀疑,并且与标准流感测试没有区别。政府报告事件而不是病例数。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Is bird flu infecting humans? How would we know? - The Conversation

News Brief
The article discusses the current situation of bird flu (H5N1) in Australia, where the virus is circulating among birds, including migratory and local wild birds. As of August 16, 2026, no human cases of the currently circulating strain, 2.3.4.4b, have been reported in Australia, though there was one case of a related H5N1 strain (2.3.2.1a) in a child who returned from overseas travel. The Australian Centre for Disease Control assesses the risk to humans as low, emphasizing that while humans can contract bird flu through close contact with infected birds, the virus does not spread between humans. Public health authorities are actively monitoring for potential human infections and have established surveillance systems in hospitals and general practices. They also advise individuals who may have been exposed to report sick or dead birds and take precautions such as handwashing and using appropriate personal protective equipment. The article highlights the importance of specific testing for bird flu and the need for vigilance among high-risk groups, including wildlife carers and poultry workers.
Original source text (English)
Search analysis, research, academics... Home Arts + Culture Economy Education Environment + Energy Ethics + Religion Health Politics + Society Science + Tech World Podcasts Local En Español Edition Africa Australia Brasil Canada Canada (français) Català España Europe France Global Indonesia New Zealand United Kingdom United States Donate Skip to content Edition: United States Africa Australia Brasil Canada Canada (français) Català España Europe France Global Indonesia New Zealand United Kingdom s Donate Newsletters Become an author Sign up as a reader Sign in Academic rigor, journalistic flair Arts + Culture Economy Education Environment + Energy Ethics + Religion Health Politics + Society Science + Tech World Podcasts Local Christopher Hopkins/AAP Is bird flu infecting humans? How would we know? Published: August 16, 2026 4:05pm EDT Bird flu is circulating among birds on the Australian mainland. So with the confirmed number of detections growing, you may be wondering about the risk of birds infecting humans. So far in 2026, no humans in Australia have been diagnosed with the currently circulating strain. And evidence from overseas indicates that in the rare situation this would occur, illness would generally be mild. But not everyone with flu-like symptoms gets tested. And even if they did, a general flu test cannot distinguish between bird flu and seasonal flu. So how would we know if a human caught bird flu from an infected bird? What is bird flu? What’s changed? Bird flu (or avian influenza) refers to strains of flu viruses that mainly affect birds. The current strain of concern is known as H5N1. Since 2020, a sub-strain of H5N1, known as 2.3.4.4b, has been circulating around the world. This has resulted in the deaths of millions of birds (including poultry). It has also crossed into other species, including dairy cows in the United States, and marine mammals. Distressingly, mass mortality events – where many animals die suddenly – have been reported. In June, the first cases of H5N1 influenza were reported in Australia, first in migratory birds, then in local wild birds. Now, the government does not report case numbers, just events, where one or more birds may be involved. There has been at least one mass mortality event reported in Australia. So with more bird flu now circulating in Australia, can we expect more humans to be infected? Yes, humans can catch bird flu Since 2003, the World Health Organization (WHO) has reported 1,000 cases of H5N1 bird flu in humans globally, with 479 deaths. This year, it has reported seven cases of H5N1 bird flu in humans (in Cambodia, Bangladesh and India) with two deaths. However, 2.3.4.4b – the sub-strain currently circulating globally, including Australia – seems to generally cause mild disease in humans. However, there have been a few cases of more severe 2.3.4.4b disease (including two deaths in the US). To date, there have been no human cases of 2.3.4.4b in Australia. But there was one case of a related H5N1 strain, 2.3.2.1a, in a child who returned from overseas travel. Even with more 2.3.4.4b circulating in recent weeks, the Australian Centre for Disease Control still assesses the risk to humans as low. While humans can catch the virus from close contact with infected birds, the virus does not currently spread between humans. This means the risk of a significant human pandemic – which would need widespread transmission between humans – is currently low. Who’s at risk? What are the symptoms? Experience in other countries suggests most human infection results from close contact with infected animals. In Australia, the highest risk groups currently are wildlife carers, including veterinary staff and those managing sick or dead birds. But public health authorities are watching for other groups who may become at risk should the outbreak spread, such as poultry workers. Symptoms are typically mild, with a runny dose, cough and fever, and sometimes nausea and diarrhoea. Conjunctivitis (“pink eye”) is one symptom associated with bird flu that we don’t often see in seasonal flu. However, more serious disease can develop. In other countries, this has generally been associated with infections due to H5N1 strains other than 2.3.4.4b. Is there a bird flu test? What happens next? Standard flu tests should detect bird flu too. But they don’t distinguish between seasonal flu and bird flu. Detecting bird flu relies on doctors letting the lab know they suspect bird flu (for example, if a patient has had contact with birds that may be infected). So if you have flu-like symptoms and are in one of the groups more likely to be exposed to bird flu, tell your doctor so they can order the specific test for bird flu. It’s best to phone ahead, and present for testing wearing a mask. People with confirmed bird flu should be monitored and follow the directions of their local public health unit. Authorities will also assess whether others may have been exposed and infected. Some people may be offered the preventive antiviral medicine oseltamivir to reduce their risk of becoming infected or to treat an infection. How else would we know? There is also surveillance for bird flu in humans. This means actively looking out for signals without relying on individuals coming forward for testing. For instance, the WHO Collaborating Centre for Reference and Research on Influenza in Melbourne tests a selection of isolates (samples) routinely sent from other laboratories. Other ways to look out for humans infected with bird flu include surveillance systems based in selected hospitals and general practices. What precautions can I take? If you work with birds, advice is available about the right type of personal protective equipment to use to minimise your risk of infection. Washing your hands is also important. A regular seasonal flu vaccine is recommended for people at risk. This is not to protect against bird flu but to prevent people becoming sick with both seasonal and bird flu viruses at the same time. If you notice sick or dead birds, or other animals, you should: avoid touching them; record your location and what you see (take photos or video, if possible); report this to the 24-hour Emergency Animal Disease Hotline on 1800 675 888. Australia; wildlife carers, veterinary staff, and those managing sick or dead birds are at highest risk.
Event
Bird flu (H5N1) is circulating among birds in Australia, with no confirmed human cases of the current strain (2.3.4.4b) reported, but one case of a related H5N1 strain (2.3.2.1a) in a child who returned from overseas travel.
Scale, impact and risk

Response, status and uncertainty

B routine priorityPublic-health news

‘It’s definitely going to happen’: NT prepares for H5N1 bird flu - NT Independent

News Brief
The Northern Territory (NT) is preparing for the arrival of the H5N1 bird flu strain, which has been detected in all six Australian states. The Environment Department, through its senior director Tony Griffiths, has warned that the strain is expected to reach the NT soon, emphasizing it as the biggest national disease event for wildlife. Efforts include sampling faeces and water supplies, as well as relying on bird enthusiasts to monitor for signs of contamination. Dr Griffiths noted that the NT has 450 bird species, 37 of which are threatened, but vaccination of native species is not planned due to logistical challenges. Dr Sue Fitzpatrick, the NT chief veterinary officer, urged backyard poultry farmers to take preventive measures, such as enclosing bird runs and protecting feed and water sources from wild birds. The situation remains under investigation, with no confirmed cases yet reported in the NT. The response is focused on surveillance and community awareness rather than direct intervention.
Original source text (English)
The highly contagious H5N1 strain of bird flu is expected to reach the Northern Territory soon, the Environment Department has said, with staff sampling faeces and water supplies to identify contamination. The ABC reported NT Department of Lands, Planning and Environment senior director of species management Tony Griffiths said combating H5N1 in the NT would largely be based on surveying areas such as the Top End coastline and Barkly lakes and using bird enthusiasts to be on the lookout. “It’s definitely going to happen. It could be weeks, it could be months, but it’s a national event, it’s the biggest national disease event that has ever occurred in Australia for wildlife,” Dr Griffiths told ABC News. The flu was first detected in Western Australia in late June and has now been found in all six states. Dr Griffiths said department staff had been sampling faeces and water supplies to identify contamination, but they were not going to vaccinate native species. “We have 450 species of bird in the Territory and 37 of those are threatened, but we don’t rely on captive breeding programs to support the management of those species,” Dr Griffiths said. “The threatened birds we have are dispersed over very large areas and it’s very difficult to catch them to vaccinate.” NT chief veterinary officer Sue Fitzpatrick told the ABC backyard poultry farmers and other bird owners need to watch out for the disease. “We’re looking for bird keepers to protect their birds from wild birds, enclose their runs and put some roof over their runs, protect feed and water sources from wild birds,” Dr Fitzpatrick said
View five news elements
Time
The Environment Department has stated that the H5N1 strain is expected to reach the Northern Territory soon. The flu was first detected in Western Australia in late June and has now been found in all six states. Dr Griffiths mentioned it could be weeks or months before it occurs, but it is described as a national event.
Location and population
The Northern Territory (NT) is the focus, particularly areas such as the Top End coastline and Barkly lakes. The affected population includes native bird species, with 450 species in the Territory and 37 of them being threatened.
Event
The NT Department of Lands, Planning and Environment has warned about the imminent arrival of the H5N1 bird flu strain. Dr Griffiths stated that it is a national event and the biggest disease event in Australia for wildlife. The Environment Department is conducting sampling of faeces and water supplies to identify contamination.
Scale, impact and risk
The H5N1 strain is described as highly contagious and has already been detected in all six states of Australia. The risk is emphasized as significant for wildlife, with 37 threatened bird species in the NT. The department is not planning to vaccinate native species due to the difficulty in catching them.
Response, status and uncertainty
They are relying on bird enthusiasts to monitor for the disease. Dr Fitzpatrick advised backyard poultry farmers to protect their birds from wild birds by enclosing their runs and securing feed and water sources. The situation is under investigation and monitoring.

超过 5000 只小企鹅将接受疫苗接种以预防禽流感。这将如何保护他们? - 对话

More than 5,000 little penguins will be vaxxed against bird flu. How will this protect them? - The Conversation
新闻简报澳大利亚维多利亚州发起了世界上最大的针对 H5N1 禽流感 野生鸟类 疫苗接种 活动,目标是菲利普岛和圣基尔达的 5,000 多只小企鹅。州政府主导的这项工作使用了灭活的 H5N1 疫苗,每只鸟需要注射两剂,这一策略此前尚未在小企鹅身上进行过测试。菲利普岛已确认一例 H5N1 死亡病例,该岛约 40,000 只企鹅的栖息地由于密集的生活条件和多种环境压力而面临更高的风险。野生动物保护官员正在捕获这些鸟类并为其接种疫苗,但后勤方面的挑战仍然存在,包括需要重复捕获。专家指出,虽然 疫苗 可以减少影响,但 H5N1 很可能会进化,需要持续的 疫苗 开发。敦促公众不要触摸生病或死亡的鸟类,并向紧急动物疾病热线报告发现情况。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

More than 5,000 little penguins will be vaxxed against bird flu. How will this protect them? - The Conversation

News Brief
Victoria, Australia, has launched the world’s largest wild-bird vaccination campaign against H5N1 avian influenza, targeting more than 5,000 little penguins at Phillip Island and St Kilda. The state government-led effort uses an inactivated H5N1 vaccine that requires two doses per bird, a strategy previously untested on little penguins. One fatality from H5N1 has already been confirmed at Phillip Island, where colonies of around 40,000 penguins face heightened risk due to dense living conditions and multiple environmental stressors. Wildlife officers are capturing and vaccinating the birds, but logistical challenges remain, including the need for repeat captures. Experts note that while vaccines can reduce impact, H5N1 is likely to evolve, requiring ongoing vaccine development. The public is urged not to touch sick or dead birds and to report sightings to the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline.
Original source text (English)

How will this protect them? Published: August 18, 2026 1:18am EDT A major push is underway to catch and vaccinate more than 5,000 little penguins in Victoria to protect them against the virulent H5N1 bird flu. Victorian wildlife officers will carry out vaccinations at little penguin colonies at Phillip Island and St Kilda, in what the state government says is the world’s largest program to target wild birds. One little penguin at Phillip Island has already died of bird flu. Capturing wildlife for vaccination is difficult, particularly because they may need two or more doses of the vaccine. So, how will this unprecedented program help little penguins? A significant threat Little penguins (Eudyptula minor) are the world’s smallest penguin, reaching a height of about 30 centimetres and weighing around 1.5 kilograms. These charismatic birds live on the southern coast of Western Australia, South Australia, Victoria and New South Wales, as well as Tasmania and New Zealand. Famously, they can be found on Philip Island in Victoria, where more than 700,000 people come each year to see them emerge from the ocean at sunset and waddle up the beach to their burrows in the “penguin parade”. The colonies at Philip Island and St Kilda are home to around 40,000 penguins. Because large numbers of birds live close together and the virus is highly infectious, bird flu poses a significant threat. These little penguins live in burrows in the coastal health at Phillip Island. Goddard_Photography/Getty Capturing wildlife to vaccinate them is difficult, particularly because the bird flu vaccination requires they be caught twice, for two doses. However, little penguins are flightless, live in colonies and return at night to the same burrow, making them easier to capture and vaccinate. Birds affected by H5N1 may be unable to stand or fly, or may appear lethargic. They may have an unusual head or neck posture, or die suddenly in large numbers without any signs at all. Little penguins are already under pressure from invasive predators such as cats and foxes, artificial light, roads and overfishing. Climate change is also taking a toll, with warming oceans reducing fish numbers. A popular tourist attraction in Bicheno, Tasmania, recently had to temporarily close due to adults failing to return to shore and abandoning chicks. Ecologists believed it was linked to a sudden rise in sea temperature. Other birds also being vaccinated While the scale of Victoria’s vaccination program of wild birds is unprecedented, New Zealand – the land of birds – is also vaccinating around 300 captive (or flightless) individuals from five species. These include the critically endangered kākāpō (Strigops habroptilus), and endangered South Island takahē (Porphyrio hochstetteri). Endangered captive birds in Australian zoos are being vaccinated too, including the regent honeyeater (Anthochaera phrygia), orange-bellied parrot (Neophema chrysogaster) and Western Australia’s black cockatoos. The federal government recently authorised the use of H5N1 vaccines for priority native bird species. The inactivated viral vaccine (made of dead virus particles) had not been tested on any native birds, much less little penguins, until recently. Why two doses are needed Just like many of the vaccines we humans receive, this H5N1 vaccine requires a booster shot. After the initial vaccination, the vaccinated animal’s immune system can detect foreign viral particles and develop antibodies. The first vaccine shot also produces specialised cells called memory B cells, which remember the protein makeup of the virus when they meet it again. A second or booster vaccine is often required as part of a vaccination regime, typically after several weeks. This leads to a rapid increase in the number of antibody molecules in the blood because the memory B cells recognise the virus and trigger a wave of antibody production. Having higher levels of antibodies in the blood increases the chances of them locating a virus in the blood stream, ultimately stopping the virus infecting the penguin. Vaccines essential to response Vaccines are not guaranteed to prevent an infection every time, and we cannot vaccinate all the penguins. But they are an important tools we can use to reduce the impact of bird flu on precious wildlife, and protect other animals. The bird flu virus will continue to evolve and adapt to outsmart the host immune systems, much like COVID. Climate change will put even more stress on wildlife and induce virus adaption. Over time, new vaccines will need to be developed for new virus strains. For now, bird flu is here and vaccines are an essential part of the fight. Please not approach or touch birds that are unwell or dead. Report to the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline (1800 675 888). Vaccination Avian flu H5N1 Environment Avian flu Bird flu Want to write? Event
Victoria’s state government is conducting the world’s largest vaccination program against H5N1 avian influenza in wild little penguins, using an inactivated viral vaccine requiring two doses per bird.
Scale, impact and risk
One confirmed death of a little penguin from H5N1 reported at Phillip Island; large colonies (~40,000 penguins) at high risk due to high infectivity and close living conditions; penguins already stressed by invasive predators, artificial light, roads, overfishing, and climate-driven reductions in fish.
Response, status and uncertainty
Vaccination program ongoing; wildlife officers capturing and vaccinating penguins; vaccine not yet tested on little penguins prior to this program; requires two doses with weeks between; vaccines reduce impact but cannot prevent all infections; virus expected to evolve, necessitating future vaccine updates; public advised not to approach sick or dead birds and to report sightings to the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline.

菲利普岛确认澳大利亚首例小企鹅感染 H5 禽流感死亡 - 卫报

First Australian death of little penguin with H5 bird flu confirmed on Phillip Island - The Guardian
新闻简报澳大利亚在维多利亚州菲利普岛的一只小企鹅身上确认了首例 H5 禽流感 病例,这是该国首次有记录的该物种因该病毒死亡。这一发现引发了一项紧急 疫苗接种 运动,目标是菲利普岛和墨尔本圣基尔达海滩上的多达 5,000 只小企鹅,服药间隔数周。当局还报告在维多利亚州的大凤头燕鸥中检测到另外两例 H5 病例,在新南威尔士州检测到第五例病例,引发了人们对 野生鸟类 人群中更广泛传播的担忧。虽然受感染的企鹅是在受欢迎的“企鹅游行”区域外发现的,但专家警告说,如果不能及时建立免疫力,大约 40,000 只企鹅的栖息地将面临重大风险。维多利亚州政府强调了该物种的标志性地位,并承诺在 疫苗接种 工作开始时优先提供资源来保护该群体。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

First Australian death of little penguin with H5 bird flu confirmed on Phillip Island - The Guardian

News Brief
Australia confirmed its first case of H5 avian influenza in a little penguin on Phillip Island, Victoria, marking the country’s first recorded death of the species from the virus. The discovery has triggered an emergency vaccination campaign targeting up to 5,000 little penguins across Phillip Island and Melbourne’s St Kilda beach, with doses spaced weeks apart. Authorities also reported two additional H5 detections in greater crested terns in Victoria and a fifth case in New South Wales, raising concerns about wider spread among wild bird populations. While the infected penguin was found outside the popular ‘penguin parade’ area, experts warn the colony of roughly 40,000 birds faces significant risk if immunity cannot be established in time. The Victorian government emphasized the iconic status of the species and pledged priority resources to protect the colony as vaccination efforts begin.
