Photograph: ozflash/Getty Images/iStockphoto First Australian death of little penguin with H5 bird flu confirmed on Phillip Island Authorities confirm first Australian death of species found with virus near where it attracts crowds to a ‘penguin parade’ Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast Australia’s first case of the deadly H5 bird flu in a little penguin has been confirmed on Victoria’s Phillip Island, only days after the state government announced it would begin vaccinating the species. Confirmation of the virus in the little penguin will spark a race to vaccinate thousands of birds, with the government announcing on Saturday it would begin the rollout on Monday. The government did not provide the exact location where the bird was found, but said it was not inside the island’s famous penguin parade area. A spokesperson confirmed the little penguin had died. There are about 40,000 little penguins on Phillip Island, making it the world’s biggest colony of the species and a major tourist attraction. Bird flu has been evolving and migrating for 30 years. It is a far more deadly virus that has now arrived in Australia Read more Environment advocates have been concerned about the potential for mass deaths of little penguins if the virus reached the island. Euan Ritchie, a professor in wildlife ecology and conservation at Deakin university, said the detection of H5 bird flu in the much-loved little penguin population was “devastating”. “Phillip Island is home to a diverse array and significant populations of native wildlife, including Australian fur seals, eastern barred bandicoots, and recently reintroduced bush stone curlews,” he said. “Governments must provide whatever resources are needed to help wildlife survive through and recover from this bird flu outbreak.” Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email On Wednesday, the Victorian government announced plans for the world’s largest H5 bird flu vaccination program of wild birds. The government said it would vaccinate up to 5,000 little penguins across populations at Phillip Island and at Melbourne’s St Kilda beach. While vaccinating wild birds is challenging, it was considered possible in Victoria’s wild little penguins because they generally return to the same burrows at Phillip Island and St Kilda each night. But vaccination requires the delivery of two doses with a gap of a few weeks between each dose. The detection on Phillip Island will spark concern that it may be too late for some little penguins to develop immunity. Victoria’s chief biodiversity officer, James Todd, said he understood Victorians would be worried about the detection of the deadly virus in a little penguin. “We know that the detection in a little penguin on Phillip Island is of concern,” he said. skip past newsletter promotion after newsletter promotion “But we are proceeding with vaccination of the little penguin colony on Phillip Island and at St Kilda as a meaningful way in which it can help mitigate the impact of H5 on those populations. “We know that little penguins are iconic and highly valued by Victorians, and for that reason, we are working as a priority to protect those colonies.” The state’s chief veterinary officer, Dr Graeme Cooke, said Agriculture Victoria and Phillip Island Nature Parks “activated plans” to vaccinate little penguins as soon as the federal government authorised the vaccines for priority native bird species. “Agriculture Victoria is working closely with Phillip Island Nature Parks to support vaccination of little penguins from Monday,” he said. “While the first detection in a little penguin is a concern, vaccination of little penguins on Phillip Island remains a meaningful way to help protect this important colony.” The Victorian government said there had been two further detections of H5 in greater crested terns, one at Lakes Entrance and the other at Dromana on the Mornington peninsula. Todd said the Lakes Entrance detection was significant because it showed the virus was continuing to move through wild bird populations across Victoria. On Saturday, the NSW government also confirmed the fifth detected case in the state, in a tern found at Eden on the far south coast. The government said it would increase surveillance in the area as a result. There have been two other confirmed cases in local birds in the state, one at Narooma on the state’s south coast and one at Wentworth in the far south-west. All of the confirmed cases in local birds have been in greater crested terns. 澳大利亚推出针对小企鹅群体的 禽流感 疫苗接种计划 - MercoPress

Australia launches program to vaccinate colonies of little penguins against avian influenza - MercoPress
新闻简报在确认一只企鹅死于该病毒后,澳大利亚启动了一项计划,为小企鹅群体接种 禽流感 H5 病毒株疫苗。MercoPress 于 2026 年 8 月 19 日报道,该计划涉及在维多利亚州菲利普岛和圣基尔达捕获 5,000 多只鸟类并为其接种疫苗。行动是在夜间企鹅从海里返回时进行的,它们被引导到一个特殊区域进行 疫苗接种 和微芯片植入。该病毒大约两周半前首次在维多利亚州西南部被发现,随后传播到菲利普岛。菲利普岛上的鸟类栖息地多达 40,000 只,是世界上最大的鸟类栖息地之一。2025 年 5 月的一项州政府研究模拟了 H5N1 的风险,预测在最坏的情况下将有多达 16,000 只企鹅死亡。维多利亚州首席生物多样性官 James Todd 强调了快速、安全的 疫苗接种 计划的必要性,并指出由于企鹅的筑巢习惯,该病毒对企鹅构成了重大威胁。该计划优先考虑参与旅游“企鹅游行”的企鹅,因为它们更习惯于人类互动。目前,夜间旅游企鹅观察活动将继续进行,官员们正在考虑扩大该计划,将圈养的其他脆弱动物物种也纳入其中。

该计划于 2026 年 8 月 19 日启动,行动计划在夜间企鹅从海上返回时进行。该病毒于大约两周半前,即 2026 年 7 月 25 日首次被发现。
地点与对象
疫苗接种 项目正在澳大利亚维多利亚州的菲利普岛和圣基尔达岛进行。菲利普岛上的鸟类栖息地多达 40,000 只,是世界上最大的鸟类栖息地之一。
事件
澳大利亚野生动物保护机构启动了一项计划,为小企鹅接种 禽流感 H5 菌株疫苗。在 疫苗接种 活动前夕,一只小企鹅被证实死于该病毒。

该病毒已传播到菲利普岛,州政府的一项研究模拟了 H5N1 的风险,预测在最坏的情况下可能有 8,000 至 16,000 只企鹅死亡。该计划的目标是在未来几周内为 5,000 多只鸟接种疫苗。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗接种 计划在夜间进行,以尽量减少对企鹅的干扰。限制工作人员数量以降低生物安全风险。该计划被视为优先事项,专家建议尽快、安全地继续实施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia launches program to vaccinate colonies of little penguins against avian influenza - MercoPress

News Brief
Australia has launched a program to vaccinate colonies of little penguins against the H5 strain of avian influenza, following the confirmation of a penguin's death from the virus. The initiative, reported by MercoPress on August 19, 2026, involves capturing and vaccinating over 5,000 birds on Philip Island and St. Kilda in Victoria. Operations are conducted at night when penguins return from the sea, and they are directed to a special area for vaccination and microchipping. The virus was first detected in southwestern Victoria about two and a half weeks ago and has since spread to Philip Island. The colony on Philip Island, which numbers up to 40,000 birds, is one of the largest in the world. A state government study from May 2025 modeled the risks of H5N1, predicting up to 16,000 penguin deaths in a worst-case scenario. Victoria’s Chief Biodiversity Officer, James Todd, emphasized the need for a rapid and safe vaccination program, noting that the virus poses a significant threat to the penguins due to their nesting habits. The program prioritizes penguins involved in the tourist 'penguin parade' as they are more accustomed to human interaction. The nightly tourist penguin watch will continue for now, and officials are considering expanding the program to include other vulnerable animal species in captivity.

South Atlantic News Agency MercoPress, en Español Montevideo, August 20th 2026 - 20:56 UTC A personalized mRNA therapy passes a phase 3 melanoma trial for the first time Harry and Meghan will move to the UK without resuming official royal duties Falklands consolidates the future of its economy in the fishing and hydrocarbons industries Discover more War & Conflict Multilateral Organizations political News Geographic Reference History Newspapers Latinos & Latin-Americans Politics news Australia launches program to vaccinate colonies of little penguins against avian influenza Wednesday, August 19th 2026 - 08:45 UTC Full article 0 comments Operations will be conducted at night when the penguins return from the sea and are directed to a special area where they will receive the first dose According to ABC News Australia wildlife conservation agencies in the state of Victoria have launched a program to vaccinate little penguins against the H5 strain of avian influenza. Over the next few weeks, experts plan to capture and vaccinate more than 5,000 birds in colonies on Philip Island and St. Kilda. The virus, first detected in southwestern Victoria about two and a half weeks ago, has spread to Philip Island. On the eve of the vaccination campaign, the death of a little penguin from the virus was confirmed. The bird was found in a different part of the island, away from the site of the nightly tourist penguin watch. The colony on Phillip Island numbers up to 40,000 birds and is one of the largest in the world. A state government study released in May 2025 modeled the risks of H5N1 to this population and predicted that, in a worst-case scenario, between 8,000 and 16,000 penguins could die. Little penguins are vulnerable to the spread of infection because they nest and gather in large groups. Victoria’s Chief Biodiversity Officer, James Todd, stated that operations will be conducted at night. When the penguins return from the sea, they will be directed to a special area where they will receive their first dose of the vaccine and be microchipped, after which the birds will be able to return to their burrows. The number of staff will be limited to reduce biosafety risks to both people and animals. According to Todd, vaccinating wild animals is complicated by the need for two doses of the vaccine, which is usually impractical or impossible. Priority will be given to penguins participating in the tourist “penguin parade”: they are accustomed to people and have predictable movement patterns. The nightly shows for visitors will continue for now. Jack Gough, a representative of the Invasive Species Council, called Philip Island likely the best-prepared location in Australia, but noted that vaccination should have begun earlier. Todd emphasized that the detection of the virus in the little penguin is cause for concern, but experts advise continuing the vaccination program as quickly and safely as possible. Officials are also considering expanding the program to include vulnerable animal species in captivity. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

H5N1 禽流感对澳大利亚和新西兰独特野生动物构成的威胁 - NPR

The threat the H5N1 avian flu poses to Australia and New Zealand’s unique wildlife - NPR
新闻简报The H5N1 avian flu virus has arrived in Australia and New Zealand, threatening their unique wildlife. Experts warn that the virus could push some native species to extinction, with a recent Australian government assessment indicating that nearly 1 in 5 native birds and mammals are at high, very high, or extremely high risk. The virus has already affected a little penguin colony in Bayside, Melbourne, and is spreading rapidly. Conservationists emphasize that while biosecurity measures are being strengthened and vaccination programs are being considered, the vaccine alone may not be enough to protect these species. The Australian government acknowledges the risk and states that mass wildlife deaths are now inevitable, less than two months after the first mainland case was detected. The situation highlights the vulnerability of endemic species to this highly contagious pathogen.

澳大利亚和新西兰,重点关注本土鸟类和哺乳动物物种,包括墨尔本贝赛德的小企鹅栖息地。

规模、影响与风险
A recent Australian government assessment found nearly 1 in 5 native birds and mammals are at high, very high, or extremely high risk from the virus. Of the more than 2,000 species examined, several are already critically endangered, and hundreds are at risk of severe depletion. The virus could accelerate the extinction of species unique to these regions.

全国各地正在加强生物安全措施。澳大利亚计划效仿新西兰的做法,为一些圈养的本土鸟类接种疫苗。然而,专家表示,仅靠 疫苗 不足以保护本地物种免受病毒侵害。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

The threat the H5N1 avian flu poses to Australia and New Zealand’s unique wildlife - NPR

News Brief
The H5N1 avian flu virus has arrived in Australia and New Zealand, threatening their unique wildlife. Experts warn that the virus could push some native species to extinction, with a recent Australian government assessment indicating that nearly 1 in 5 native birds and mammals are at high, very high, or extremely high risk. The virus has already affected a little penguin colony in Bayside, Melbourne, and is spreading rapidly. Conservationists emphasize that while biosecurity measures are being strengthened and vaccination programs are being considered, the vaccine alone may not be enough to protect these species. The Australian government acknowledges the risk and states that mass wildlife deaths are now inevitable, less than two months after the first mainland case was detected. The situation highlights the vulnerability of endemic species to this highly contagious pathogen.
Original source text (English)

Animals The threat the H5N1 avian flu poses to Australia and New Zealand's unique wildlife August 18, 20265:25 PM ET Heard on All Things Considered By Kristina Kukolja The threat the H5N1 avian flu poses to Australia and New Zealand’s unique wildlife Listen· 2:342-Minute ListenPlaylist Transcript Toggle more options Download Embed Embed <iframe src="https://www.npr.org/player/embed/nx-s1-5929107/nx-s1-9892129" width="100%" height="290" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="NPR embedded audio player"> Transcript After killing millions of animals worldwide, the highly contagious H5N1 avian flu has arrived in Australia and New Zealand. Experts warn it could push some unique and native species to extinction. Transcript JUANA SUMMERS, HOST: After killing millions of animals worldwide, the highly contagious H5N1 avian flu has arrived in Australia and New Zealand. Experts warn it could push some unique and native species to extinction. Kristina Kukolja reports from Melbourne. (SOUNDBITE OF WATER LAPPING) KRISTINA KUKOLJA, BYLINE: Gentle waves lap the shore at sunset in Bayside, Melbourne, to reveal one of the city's cutest attractions. (SOUNDBITE OF PENGUIN CALLING) KUKOLJA: The breakwater at the end of the main pier is home to some 1,400 little penguins. Ecologist Tiana Preston is among experts warning the deadly H5N1 bird flu virus that's been spreading in Australia in the last several weeks could wipe out up to 40% of their colony. TIANA PRESTON: It's that sort of behavior where they're coming into contact with each other a lot that makes them particularly vulnerable. KUKOLJA: A recent Australian government assessment has found nearly 1 in 5 native birds and mammals are at high, very high or extremely high risk from the virus. Of the more than 2,000 species examined, several are already critically endangered, and, the report states, unlikely to survive infection, and hundreds are at risk of being severely depleted. CAROL BOOTH: We could have a greatly accelerated push to extinction for a lot of species. KUKOLJA: That's Carol Booth, a conservation and biosecurity policy expert with the group Invasive Species Australia. BOOTH: Eighty-seven percent of our mammals are found nowhere else and almost half our bird species, which means if we lose them here, they're gone globally. KUKOLJA: A risk acknowledged by the Australian government, which says mass wildlife deaths are now inevitable, less than two months after the first mainland case was detected. Biosecurity measures are being tightened around the country. Australia will also follow New Zealand's lead and vaccinate some native birds in captivity, says Environment Minister Murray Watt. MURRAY WATT: We're taking this step now because, of course, the situation regarding bird flu in Australia is escalating. (SOUNDBITE OF BIRD SQUEAKING) KUKOLJA: New Zealand aims to vaccinate 300 wild and captive birds from the country's five most threatened species, like the flightless nocturnal kakapo parrot. (SOUNDBITE OF PARROT SQUAWKING) KUKOLJA: Ash Murphy is a biosecurity manager with the New Zealand government's Department of Conservation. ASH MURPHY: The risk in New Zealand is some of our species are so low that a decline might be impossible to recover from. KUKOLJA: But experts say the vaccine alone is not enough to defend native species against the virus, and more needs to be done. For NPR News, I'm Kristina Kukolja in Melbourne, Australia. This incident highlights the potential threat of avian influenza to local bird populations and underscores the need for further investigation and monitoring.

禽流感可能杀死了加州大学伯克利分校的猎鹰,但希望它们重获新生 - Daily Cal

Bird flu may have killed UC Berkeley’s falcons, but hope for their return lives - Daily Cal
新闻简报《加州日报》于 2026 年 8 月 20 日发表的文章讨论了禽流感对在钟楼筑巢的加州大学伯克利分校游隼的潜在影响。监控猎鹰的摄像机三个月前坏了,引发了人们对它们生存的担忧。负责 24 小时直播的加州猎鹰队希望看到猎鹰回归,尽管禽流感的并发症仍在持续。据报道,该事件为疑似事件,但没有提供确诊病例数或死亡人数。应对措施包括监测工作和康复希望,但对猎鹰状况的调查仍在进行中。该文章没有提供明确的风险级别或有关禽流感对猎鹰健康影响的进一步细节。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于 2026 年 8 月 20 日。证据中没有明确说明事件日期,但相机在发表日期前三个月(即 2026 年 5 月 20 日左右)坏了。
地点与对象
该事件发生在加州大学伯克利分校,特别涉及筑巢在钟楼上的游隼。受影响的种群包括猎鹰(鸟类)以及监测它们的科学家和志愿者。
事件
三个月前坏掉的摄像机表明,禽流感可能杀死了加州大学伯克利分校的猎鹰。该事件由负责 24 小时直播的组织 Cal Falcons 报道,并被描述为猎鹰死亡的可疑原因。
规模、影响与风险
文章提到了禽流感持续出现的并发症,但没有提供具体的病例数、死亡人数或接触人数。没有明确说明风险,但流感被认为是对猎鹰的潜在威胁。
应对、状态与不确定性
回应包括对猎鹰回归的希望以及科学家和志愿者的持续监测。猎鹰死亡原因的调查尚未完全解决,情况仍在调查中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu may have killed UC Berkeley’s falcons, but hope for their return lives - Daily Cal

News Brief
The article from Daily Cal, published on August 20, 2026, discusses the potential impact of bird flu on UC Berkeley’s peregrine falcons, which nest on the Campanile. The cameras monitoring the falcons broke three months prior, raising concerns about their survival. Cal Falcons, the organization responsible for the 24-hour livestream, hopes to see the falcons return despite ongoing complications with bird flu. The event is reported as suspected, with no confirmed case counts or death numbers provided. The response includes monitoring efforts and hope for recovery, but the investigation into the falcons' condition remains under way. The article does not provide explicit risk levels or further details on the health effects of the bird flu on the falcons.

H5N1 引发澳大利亚流行病 - AOL.com

H5N1 sparks a very Australian epidemic - AOL.com
新闻简报H5N1 bird flu has reached Australia, infecting over 200 wild seabirds since June 2026 and posing a significant threat to the country’s unique wildlife, including endangered species like Tasmanian devils, quolls, and dingoes. The virus, which has been circulating globally since 2020, has already infected animals from foxes to sea lions in other regions, raising concerns about its potential to spread to Australia’s endemic species. The Australian government has initiated a vaccination campaign targeting captive species such as the orange-bellied parrot and regent honeyeater, while emphasizing the need for stringent biosecurity measures to protect the poultry industry, which contributes $4.1 billion annually to the economy. Human health risk is currently assessed as low, though the virus has caused over 1,000 infections worldwide since the late 1990s, with nearly half of those cases resulting in death. Authorities are monitoring the situation closely and have allocated $113 million for pandemic preparedness, including vaccine stockpiles.
查看新闻五要素
时间
自6月首次记录病例以来,该病毒于2022年以来迅速扩散。发布日期为2026-08-15。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,特别关注其独特的野生动物,如塔斯马尼亚袋獾、袋鼬、野狗和鸭嘴兽,以及家禽业。
事件
自 2026 年 6 月以来,H5N1 禽流感已感染澳大利亚 200 多只野生海鸟,人们担心它会蔓延到其他物种。澳大利亚政府正在准备一项 疫苗接种 运动,以保护圈养的濒危物种。
规模、影响与风险
The virus threatens nearly one in five of Australia’s native species, with hundreds of unique birds and mammals at risk of severe population reductions or extinction. The poultry industry, worth $4.1 billion annually, is also at risk. Human health risk is deemed low, but the virus has caused over 1,000 infections globally since the late 1990s, with almost half of those cases resulting in death.
应对、状态与不确定性
The Australian government is implementing a vaccination campaign and investing $113 million in preparedness activities. Biosecurity measures are emphasized as critical to prevent further spread. The situation is under active investigation, with no confirmed human cases yet reported.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

H5N1 sparks a very Australian epidemic - AOL.com

News Brief
H5N1 bird flu has reached Australia, infecting over 200 wild seabirds since June 2026 and posing a significant threat to the country’s unique wildlife, including endangered species like Tasmanian devils, quolls, and dingoes. The virus, which has been circulating globally since 2020, has already infected animals from foxes to sea lions in other regions, raising concerns about its potential to spread to Australia’s endemic species. The Australian government has initiated a vaccination campaign targeting captive species such as the orange-bellied parrot and regent honeyeater, while emphasizing the need for stringent biosecurity measures to protect the poultry industry, which contributes $4.1 billion annually to the economy. Human health risk is currently assessed as low, though the virus has caused over 1,000 infections worldwide since the late 1990s, with nearly half of those cases resulting in death. Authorities are monitoring the situation closely and have allocated $113 million for pandemic preparedness, including vaccine stockpiles.
Original source text (English)
H5N1 bird flu has reached Australia’s shores and is threatening to infect some of the country’s most unique wildlife. Australasia is the last continent to be hit by the highly infectious avian influenza H5N1 , a new strain of which has been circulating worldwide since 2020. Since recording its first case in June, more than 200 wild seabirds in Australia have been infected, with fears that the highly infectious flu could quickly ‘jump’ to other species. While H5N1 has infected animals from foxes to sea lions in other parts of the world in recent years, the epidemic now threatens some of Australia’s most iconic wildlife – many of which are found nowhere else on earth. An Australian government assessment of more than 2,000 native species warned that nearly one in five faces a high risk from the deadly H5N1 bird flu, with hundreds of unique birds and mammals vulnerable to severe population reductions or even extinction. Those animals include iconic species like the Tasmanian devils, quolls, and dingoes. Other aquatic mammals, including the platypus, which is only found in Australia, are at risk of infection through contact with bird droppings in shared wetlands and coastal environments. Tasmanian devils are an endangered species - HANDOUT/Aussie Ark/AFP via Getty Images Dingoes are among the mammals that are unique to Australia - iStockphoto “This could be quite devastating to what is a unique ecosystem with endangered populations,” Professor Ian Brown, Head of Avian Virology at The Pirbright Institute, told The Telegraph. “It’s been a waiting game [...] I don’t think that any are that surprised that the virus has got to Australia now,” Prof Ashley C. Banyard, an avian virology expert at the government’s Animal and Plant Health Agency, said. “I think people have been expecting it [the virus] to reach Australia as several bird species that travel to that region have long-distance migratory pathways from areas where the virus has been detected previously, and we continue to detect positives on British Overseas Territories in the sub-Antarctic region where these species migrate from”, said Prof Banyard. The government is set to begin a vaccination campaign in the coming weeks to protect some of these endangered species that are conserved in captivity – beginning with rare birds like the orange-bellied parrot, the regent honeyeater, and Western Australia’s black cockatoos. “We’ve been very clear that we cannot stop the H5 bird flu from spreading amongst wild birds, but we can take action to protect the species most at risk, and the vaccination is now part of that response,” Australia’s Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said in a statement on Tuesday. “Obviously, Australia wants to protect these species; they’re of high conservation concern, and they want to avoid this virus ripping through them,” said Prof Banyard. “But I think the bigger picture is how do you deal with the introductions from wild birds, which we’ve all suffered with for many years across Europe and elsewhere. “Ultimately biosecurity is essential to prevention in poultry species and with wild bird cases increasing in the region, stringent biosecurity will be critical in preventing this becoming an even bigger problem,” he added. Since 2022, H5N1 has spread at a rapid pace across the world, decimating millions of domestic and wild bird populations as well as infecting a wide range of animals, from otters to seals to lions, tigers, and bears. It has had a huge impact on the farming industry and sent the price of eggs and poultry meat soaring as flocks are culled to try to contain the spread of the virus. In Britain and the EU, almost 48 million birds were culled between 2021 and 2022 alone at the height of the bird-flu season. This risk now threatens the Australian poultry industry, which contributes roughly $4.1 billion to the regional agricultural industry every year. “It hasn’t hit poultry [in Australia] yet. If it does get into flocks, it’s always going to be problematic. But I would imagine flocks in Australia and New Zealand are quite spread apart, and so the risk of lateral spread is probably lower [than in the UK],” said Prof Banyard. The virus also poses a threat to human health, and it has long been feared that H5N1 could spark the next pandemic, should it develop the ability to spread between people. More than 1,000 people have contracted the virus since it first emerged in the late 1990s, mostly through close contact with sickened animals, almost half of whom have died. Australian authorities currently deem the risk to human health as “low”, and have invested $113 million Australian dollars (£59 million) into preparedness activities, including boosting national stockpiles of pandemic flu vaccines, should the virus gain the ability to spread person-to-person. Protect yourself and your family by learning more about Global Health Security

Event
H5N1 bird flu has infected over 200 wild seabirds in Australia since June 2026, with fears of it spreading to other species. The Australian government is preparing a vaccination campaign to protect endangered species in captivity.
Scale, impact and risk
The virus threatens nearly one in five of Australia’s native species, with hundreds of unique birds and mammals at risk of severe population reductions or extinction. The poultry industry, worth $4.1 billion annually, is also at risk. Human health risk is deemed low, but the virus has caused over 1,000 infections globally since the late 1990s, with almost half of those cases resulting in death.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Australian government is implementing a vaccination campaign and investing $113 million in preparedness activities. Biosecurity measures are emphasized as critical to prevent further spread. The situation is under active investigation, with no confirmed human cases yet reported.

In South Australia, more than a thousand terns may have died from H5N1 - UA.NEWS
新闻简报在南澳大利亚,已有一千多只凤头燕鸥被发现死亡,初步估计它们可能死于 H5N1 禽流感。该州州长 Peter Malinauskas 于 2026 年 8 月 18 日星期三报告了这一发现,并表示损失令人深感震惊。约克半岛附近的特劳布里奇岛以及袋鼠岛附近的北页岛和南页岛记录了大规模鸟类死亡事件。截至报告日期,该州已记录 177 例 H5 禽流感 确诊病例,但没有报告哺乳动物大规模死亡或在家禽或牲畜中检测到 H5N1 病例。州政府正在调查袋鼠岛上一只小企鹅的疑似病例。目前情况仍在审查中,尚未确认人类感染情况,也没有有关病毒传播的进一步细节。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该事件于 2026 年 8 月 20 日报道,并于 2026 年 8 月 18 日星期三发现大规模鸟类死亡事件。

大规模鸟类死亡事件发生在约克半岛附近的特劳布里奇岛,以及南澳大利亚袋鼠岛附近的北佩奇和南佩奇两个小岛上。受影响的种群包括凤头燕鸥,袋鼠岛上的一只小企鹅出现疑似病例。
事件
超过千只凤头燕鸥被发现死亡,疑似死于H5N1 禽流感。州政府证实了这一发现,并正在调查一只小企鹅的疑似病例。
规模、影响与风险
据报道,有超过 1,000 只凤头燕鸥死亡,其中南澳大利亚州确诊了 177 例 H5 禽流感 病例。没有报告哺乳动物大规模死亡或在家禽或牲畜中检测到 H5N1 病毒的情况。州州长称这次损失令人深感震惊。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

In South Australia, more than a thousand terns may have died from H5N1 - UA.NEWS

News Brief
In South Australia, more than a thousand crested terns have been found dead, with preliminary estimates suggesting they may have died from H5N1 avian influenza. The state Premier, Peter Malinauskas, reported the discovery on Wednesday, 18 August 2026, and stated the loss was deeply alarming. The mass bird deaths were recorded on Traubridge Island near the York Peninsula and on North Page and South Page islands near Kangaroo Island. As of the report date, 177 confirmed cases of H5 avian influenza had been recorded in the state, though no mass deaths of mammals or H5N1 detection in poultry or livestock were reported. The state government is investigating the suspected case in a little penguin on Kangaroo Island. The situation remains under review, with no confirmed human infections or further details on the spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)
News World In South Australia, more than a thousand terns may have died from H5N1 Lev Shevtsov 20 August 2026 07:59 Authorities in the state of South Australia have reported three instances of mass bird deaths on remote islands. In total, more than 1,000 dead crested terns were found there, which, according to preliminary estimates, may have died from H5N1 avian influenza, ABC News Australia reports. State Premier Peter Malinauskas told the South Australian Parliament that the dead birds were discovered on Wednesday. The cases were recorded on Traubridge Island near the York Peninsula, as well as on the two small islands of North Page and South Page near Kangaroo Island. For more breaking news, follow the UA.News Telegram channel . According to Malinauskas, the mass die-off was detected by the state government’s disease surveillance teams. He called the loss of more than a thousand birds deeply alarming and noted that authorities are taking these cases seriously. As of the date of this report, 177 confirmed cases of H5 avian influenza had been recorded in South Australia. The state government is also investigating a suspected case in a little penguin on Kangaroo Island. No mass deaths of mammals have been reported, and H5N1 has not been detected in poultry or livestock. Read us on Telegram and Sends Download our app

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

1000只鸟被发现死于禽流感导致大规模死亡

1000 birds found dead in avian flu mass mortalities
新闻简报据报道,南澳大利亚发生了凤头燕鸥大规模死亡事件,已有超过 1000 只凤头燕鸥死亡。南澳大利亚州当局在向州议会提交的报告中称,怀疑死亡病例是由 H5N1 禽流感 引起的。受影响的地区包括偏远的近海岛屿,例如约克半岛附近的特鲁布里奇岛以及袋鼠岛附近的北页岛和南页岛。截至周三,野生鸟类 已确诊 236 起 H5 禽流感事件,其中大多数病例集中在南澳大利亚。自 暴发 启动以来,紧急动物疾病热线已接到超过 21,000 个电话。虽然重点仍然放在早期检测和 Z​​XQTERMHQXZ 上,但进一步的测试正在进行中,以确定新南威尔士州 400 只鹈鹕死亡的原因,这些死亡已被排除为禽流感。目前情况正在调查中,西北海岸线以外地区尚未有大规模死亡的报道。
查看新闻五要素
时间
周三报告了大规模死亡事件,当局周四发表评论。暴发 开始较早,截至周三,野生鸟类 已确认发生 236 起 H5 禽流感事件。
地点与对象
南澳大利亚,特别是偏远的近海岛屿,例如约克半岛附近的特鲁布里奇岛以及袋鼠岛附近的北页岛和南页岛。受影响的种群包括凤头燕鸥和可能的其他鸟类。
事件
据报道,凤头燕鸥大量死亡,当局怀疑原因是 H5N1。袋鼠岛一只死去的小企鹅也正在接受调查。
规模、影响与风险
1000只凤头燕鸥被发现死亡。截至周三,野生鸟类 已确认发生 236 起 H5 禽流感事件,其中大部分发生在南澳大利亚。自 暴发 启动以来,紧急动物疾病热线已接到超过 21,000 个电话。该风险并未明确说明,但当局重点关注早期发现和 监测。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局正在对西北海岸线进行检查,尚未报告其他地方出现大规模死亡事件。进一步的测试正在进行中,可能需要几周时间才能确定新南威尔士州 400 只鹈鹕死亡的原因。重点是早期检测和 Z​​XQTERMHQXZ。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

1000 birds found dead in avian flu mass mortalities

News Brief
Mass mortalities of crested terns have been reported in South Australia, with over 1000 birds found dead. South Australian authorities suspect the deaths are due to H5N1 avian influenza, as stated in a report to the state parliament. The affected areas include remote offshore islands such as Troubridge Island off Yorke Peninsula and North Page and South Page off Kangaroo Island. As of Wednesday, there were 236 confirmed H5 bird flu events in wild birds, with most cases concentrated in South Australia. Over 21,000 calls have been received by the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline since the outbreak began. While the focus remains on early detection and surveillance, further testing is underway to determine the cause of the 400 pelican deaths in NSW, which have been ruled out as bird flu. The situation is under investigation, and no mass die-offs have been reported outside the north-west coastline.
Original source text (English)
1000 birds found dead in avian flu mass mortalities Mass mortalities of crested terns have been reported in South Australia. Photo: AAP Three mass bird mortalities involving more than 1000 crested terns have been reported amid a distressing spread of avian influenza. South Australian authorities said on Thursday the coastal birds were discovered on Wednesday and were believed to have died from H5N1. Premier Peter Malinauskas told SA Parliament the deaths occurred on remote offshore islands, the ABC reports. The islands included Troubridge Island off Yorke Peninsula and two small islands off Kangaroo Island known as North Page and South Page. Another case of a dead little penguin from Kangaroo Island was also under investigation. As of Wednesday there were 236 confirmed H5 bird flu events in wild birds, with most in South Australia. Authorities have received more than 21,000 calls to the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline since the outbreak began. Tasmania’s tally has jumped to 10 after four more wild birds on King Island tested positive, with two crested terns and two silver gulls found dead on beaches and bays around Currie. Authorities say the focus remains on early detection and surveillance, with crews inspecting about 760 kilometres of the state’s north-west coastline and no mass die-offs reported. In Western Australia, wildlife experts warn the virus is already creating an animal-welfare crunch as some private veterinary clinics stop accepting birds and, in some cases, other wildlife altogether. Meanwhile, hundreds of young pelicans have been found dead at Lake Cawndilla in outback NSW. However, samples have ruled out bird flu as the cause of death. Further testing is underway but authorities say it may take weeks to determine what happened to the 400 pelicans. -with AAP Want to see more stories from The New Daily in your Google search results? - Click here to set The New Daily as a preferred source. - Tick the box next to "The New Daily". That's it

Time
Mass mortalities were reported on Wednesday, with authorities commenting on Thursday. The outbreak began earlier, with 236 confirmed H5 bird flu events in wild birds as of Wednesday.
Location and population
South Australia, specifically remote offshore islands such as Troubridge Island off Yorke Peninsula and North Page and South Page off Kangaroo Island. The affected population includes crested terns and potentially other birds.
Event
Mass mortalities of crested terns were reported, with authorities suspecting H5N1 as the cause. A dead little penguin from Kangaroo Island is also under investigation.
Scale, impact and risk
1000 crested terns were found dead. As of Wednesday, there were 236 confirmed H5 bird flu events in wild birds, with most in South Australia. Over 21,000 calls were received by the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline since the outbreak began. The risk is not explicitly stated, but authorities are focused on early detection and surveillance.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities are conducting inspections of the north-west coastline and have not reported mass die-offs elsewhere. Further testing is ongoing, and it may take weeks to determine the cause of the 400 pelican deaths in NSW. The focus is on early detection and surveillance.
